PAGE 1
The type of four-generated
numerical semigroups
with canonical reduction
Keisuke Sato
Independent Researcher, Japan
Corresponding author: stksk310@gmail.com ORCID: 0009-0005-4230-0268
Abstract
Let Γ = ⟨𝑚, 𝑛1, 𝑛2, 𝑛3⟩ be a numerical semigroup with four minimal generators, where 𝑚is its least
positive element and𝐹 its Frobenius number. Assume the canonical-reduction condition𝐹 +𝑚−𝜑 ∈
Γ for every pseudo-Frobenius number 𝜑. We prove that the type satisfies 𝑡(Γ) ≤ 4. This gives an
affirmative answer to Problem 21 of Moscariello and Sammartano, originally posed as Question 4.4
by Moscariello and Strazzanti; the bound is sharp. The proof combines a two-layer decomposition
of the Apéry set, factorization geometry in the three-generated tail, and a Euclidean descent on two
packet relations. The large polynomial positivity expansions used at the final exchange steps are
accompanied by complete static coefficient certificates and reproducible symbolic checks; the proof
itself does not rely on a finite search of numerical semigroups.
Keywords: numerical semigroup; pseudo-Frobenius number; type; embedding dimension four;
canonical reduction; Apéry set.
MSC 2020: 20M14 (primary); 20M25, 13H10 (secondary).

1 Introduction
The type of a numerical semigroup is the number of its pseudo-Frobenius numbers, and it agrees with the
Cohen–Macaulay type of the corresponding semigroup ring [10]. In this paper we control this number through
the minimal generators and their nonnegative representations. Standard structure theorems are available for
three-generated semigroups in both the symmetric and nonsymmetric cases, whereas in embedding dimension
four one must relate the three-generated subsemigroup obtained by removing the smallest generator to the gap
conditions in the original semigroup.
The condition studied here is canonical reduction. Let 𝑚 denote the least positive generator and 𝐹 the
Frobenius number. We require 𝐹 + 𝑚 − 𝜑 ∈ Γ for every pseudo-Frobenius number 𝜑. This semigroup-
theoretic formulation appears in §4 of Moscariello–Strazzanti [ 10]. The corresponding condition for all non-
negative gaps has also been studied under the name positioned [1]. We use canonical reduction as our primary
terminology and prove directly in Section 2 the passage from pseudo-Frobenius numbers to all gaps. For the
ring-theoretic background, see Rahimi [ 12].
The unrestricted embedding-dimension-four setting admits numerical semigroups of arbitrarily large type,
so a type bound of this form cannot hold without additional structure [ 3, 9]. By contrast, stronger bounds
are known in narrower Gorenstein-type classes: in embedding dimension four, the almost symmetric and
nearly Gorenstein settings lead to type at most three [ 7, 10]. Canonical reduction is broader than the nearly
Gorenstein condition [ 10]. Problem 21 of Moscariello–Sammartano asks whether this broader canonical-
reduction hypothesis nevertheless forces the sharp bound four [ 8, 9].
Our main result is the following.
1.1 T ype bound for canonical four-generated numerical semigroups
A numerical semigroup is a cofinite subset of the nonnegative integers that contains 0 and is closed under
addition. Let
Γ = ⟨𝑚, 𝑛1, 𝑛2, 𝑛3⟩, edim(Γ) = 4, 𝑚 = min(Γ ∖ {0})
with all four generators minimal; in particular, 0 < 𝑚 < 𝑛𝑟. Let 𝐹 = 𝐹 (Γ) =max(ℤ ∖ Γ)be the Frobenius
number, and define
𝑃 𝐹 (Γ) = {𝑞 ∈ ℤ ∖ Γ ∶ 𝑞 + 𝑠 ∈ Γfor every 𝑠 ∈ Γ ∖ {0}}, 𝑡(Γ) = |𝑃 𝐹 (Γ)|
Under the canonical condition
𝐹 + 𝑚 − 𝜑 ∈ Γ (∀𝜑 ∈ 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ)) (CANONICAL)
one has
𝑡(Γ) ≤ 4
The proof is assembled in § 11.2.
This gives an affirmative answer to Problem 21 of Moscariello–Sammartano, originally posed as Ques-
tion 4.4 by Moscariello–Strazzanti [ 8–10]. The bound is sharp: the canonical numerical semigroup
⟨16, 17, 19, 39⟩has type four [ 10]. Throughout the paper, the canonical condition displayed above is used at
every stage of the proof.
The proof begins with a two-layer decomposition of the Apéry set. Set𝑄(Γ) = 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ)∖{𝐹 }, and associate
to each 𝑞 ∈ 𝑄(Γ)the elements 𝑞 + 𝑚 and 𝑐𝑞 = 𝐹 + 𝑚 − 𝑞 . Their nonnegative representations imply that
𝐻 = ⟨𝑛 1, 𝑛2, 𝑛3⟩ is a genuine minimally three-generated numerical semigroup. When 𝐻 is symmetric, we
use a stable ideal and the correspondence between its minimal generators and the Apéry set to obtain the
bound.
When 𝐻 is nonsymmetric, its two pseudo-Frobenius numbers give rise to six arms. The two colors of
arms in the same direction have synchronized return levels, while three arms of the same color are excluded
using an empty relative-lattice tetrahedron and a finite path inside a box. Combining these constraints with
incomparability of the complements reduces four elements selected from a putative counterexample to one
of three configurations: a path configuration, a configuration with one singleton, or a chain configuration.
2

PAGE 4
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
The first two configurations are eliminated by comparing two returns. In the chain configuration, we
maximize one coordinate over the finite factorization set of the same element 𝑞𝐴 + 𝑚, thereby constructing a
unit-root relation. After treating the boundary and the range in which the relevant packets are available, two
exchange relations between nonnegative representations remain. We then perform a Euclidean descent that
preserves the same semigroup and the same 𝑊 = 𝐹 + 𝑚 while decreasing the positive integer 𝐸 + 𝜒. At
termination, all coefficients required for the exchange are shown to occur in representations of that same 𝑊,
and removing one copy of 𝑚 yields the contradiction 𝐹 ∈ Γ .
Sections 2–4 establish the structural reductions and the classification into the three terminal configura-
tions. Sections 5–6 eliminate the configurations involving singletons. Sections 7–10 treat the chain config-
uration, and Section 11 assembles the main theorem. Appendix A contains the complete case analysis for
the symmetric tail, Appendix B gives the relative-lattice and box-path arguments, Appendix C records the
positivity expansions, and Appendix D specifies the static full-coefficient certificates. The complete coef-
ficient tables for the two large identities are required supplementary proof data, and their coefficient checks
are computer-assisted. The argument does not rely on a finite-range scan of numerical semigroups or on a
complete formalization in a proof assistant.
2 Preliminaries and canonical reduction
We fix the hypotheses of the main theorem. In this section, the canonical condition is converted into an
involution on the Apéry set and into nonnegative representations in the supported directions. Under the
assumption of a counterexample, we also prove coprimality and minimality for the three-generated tail. The
objects passed to the later classification are the same semigroup, the same 𝐹 , 𝑚, 𝑊, and actual pseudo-
Frobenius numbers.
2.1 Common notation and scope of the contradiction argument
𝑄(Γ) = 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ) ∖ {𝐹 }, 𝑊 = 𝐹 + 𝑚, 𝐻 = ⟨𝑛 1, 𝑛2, 𝑛3⟩,
𝒜 = Ap(Γ, 𝑚) = {𝑤 ∈ Γ ∶ 𝑤 − 𝑚 ∉ Γ}.
Since 𝐹 ∈ 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ), we have 𝑡 = |𝑄(Γ)| + 1. From now on the contradiction hypothesis is |𝑄(Γ)| ≥ 4.
Selecting four rows does not mean that the total number of elements of 𝑄 is fixed to be four. We write
𝑥 ≤Γ 𝑦 when 𝑦 − 𝑥 ∈ Γ. For each 𝑞 ∈ 𝑄(Γ), define its complement by 𝑐𝑞 = 𝑊 − 𝑞.
In the nonsymmetric part we use
𝑆𝐻(𝑞) = {𝑟 ∶ 𝑞 + 𝑛𝑟 ∈ 𝐻}, 𝐷(𝑐) = {𝑟 ∶ 𝑐 − 𝑛 𝑟 ∈ 𝐻}.
The set 𝑄(Γ)and a local integer denoted by 𝑄 are distinguished by type and by the point at which they are
defined. Likewise, the two-generated semigroup 𝑇 in the symmetric case and the matrix 𝐶 in an appendix
are local notation and should not be confused with the canonical CHAIN coefficient 𝑇 = 𝑏 𝑘 + 𝛼 or the root
coefficient 𝐶.
2.2 Standard external results used in the proof
The proof uses the following three standard results as external input, in exactly the forms stated here.
These results are not proved in this paper. We retain the bibliographic information for the original sources,
and we have checked the precise locations in the supporting references used here. We do not assert unverified
theorem numbers in the historical originals.
2.2.1 Representation of symmetric three-generated semigroups
After permuting the generators, every minimally three-generated symmetric numerical semigroup can be
written as 𝐻 = ⟨𝑑𝑢, 𝑑𝑣, 𝑤⟩, with 𝑑, 𝑢, 𝑣 ≥ 2, gcd(𝑢, 𝑣) =gcd(𝑑, 𝑤) = 1, and 𝑤 ∈ ⟨𝑢, 𝑣⟩. This is the form
3

PAGE 5
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
corresponding to the three-generated complete-intersection theory of Herzog and Delorme; the direction used
here is stated explicitly in García-Sánchez–Martín Cruz, §3.2, Theorem 6 [ 2, 4, 5].
2.2.2 Standard form for nonsymmetric three-generated semigroups
For every minimally three-generated nonsymmetric numerical semigroup there exist positive integers 𝑎𝑟, 𝑏𝑟,
with 𝜌𝑟 = 𝑎𝑟 + 𝑏𝑟, such that the critical relations, the two pseudo-Frobenius numbers, and the formulas for
the primitive tail generators given below hold. The precise forms used here follow Nari–Numata–Watanabe,
§1, equation (1.1), and §2, equation (2.1.1) and Propositions 2.1–2.2 [ 5, 11]. Pairwise coprimality of the
three generators is not assumed.
2.2.3 Empty lattice tetrahedra
A lattice tetrahedron containing no lattice points other than its vertices has lattice width one. Equivalently,
there is a nonconstant primitive integer-valued affine functional that places all four vertices on two consecutive
integer levels. We use White’s theorem in this form [ 13]. Khan–Rogers, §1, Theorems 3–4, gives the normal
form required here [ 6]. The application to the relative lattice, the partition of the vertices into two pairs, and
the subsequent coefficient comparisons are all proved in this paper.
We fix the convention for 𝑆𝑇 𝐷𝐻𝐸𝑅𝑍𝑂𝐺 in a cyclic order (𝑖, 𝑗, 𝑘):
𝜌𝑖𝑛𝑖 = 𝑏𝑗𝑛𝑗 + 𝑎𝑘𝑛𝑘, 𝜌 𝑗𝑛𝑗 = 𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘, 𝜌 𝑘𝑛𝑘 = 𝑏𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑎𝑗𝑛𝑗. (HCR)
Each 𝜌𝑟 is the least positive multiple of 𝑛𝑟 admitting a nonnegative representation by the other two generators.
𝑓𝐴 = (𝜌𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑎𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 − 𝑛𝑘, 𝑓 𝐵 = (𝜌𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 − 𝑛𝑗 + (𝑏𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘, 𝑃 𝐹 (𝐻) = {𝑓𝐴, 𝑓𝐵}.
We also use the cyclic versions of these formulas. For a primitive tail,
𝑛1 = 𝑎2𝑎3 + 𝑎3𝑏2 + 𝑏2𝑏3, 𝑛 2 = 𝑎1𝑎3 + 𝑎1𝑏3 + 𝑏1𝑏3, 𝑛 3 = 𝑎1𝑎2 + 𝑎2𝑏1 + 𝑏1𝑏2.
The later cross-product comparisons retain any positive common scale.
2.3 Symmetry operations
A cyclic permutation acts simultaneously on all indices. Color reversal is the combination of an odd per-
mutation and the interchange of all 𝑎- and 𝑏-parameters. The operation reversing the pivot in PATH and the
operation interchanging 𝑗 and 𝑘 in CHAIN are given with their full parameter transformations in P5, § 7.1,
and §10.1, respectively. These operations leave Γ, 𝐹 , 𝑚, 𝑊and the set of actual pseudo-Frobenius elements
unchanged.
2.4 Discipline for representations and proof steps
An actual factorization is a representation by the generators with all coefficients nonnegative integers. A
signed equality or a completed zero identity is not, by itself, an actual factorization. Whenever the final
contradiction uses a coefficient vector, its nonnegativity is stated explicitly. Herzog criticality is applied
only after the coefficient of 𝑚 has been completely eliminated, so that the relation lies entirely in 𝐻. A
support replacement is performed only when the source occurs coefficientwise within an explicitly identified
factorization of the same actual element. Every extremal choice used below is accompanied by the underlying
finite set or well-ordering argument.
2.5 Scope of notation
For traceability to the source notation, we do not globally rename the principal symbols. The following
symbols have local scopes and should not be identified across the different rows of the table.

2.6 The two Apéry layers and supported returns
C2. Under the canonical condition of Sections 1–2, the following statements hold.
2.7 Common setting
Let Γ = ⟨𝑚, 𝑛1, 𝑛2, 𝑛3⟩ be a numerical semigroup, with 𝑚 its multiplicity, 𝐹 its Frobenius number, 𝐻 =
⟨𝑛1, 𝑛2, 𝑛3⟩, 𝑊 = 𝐹 + 𝑚 , and 𝒜 = Ap(Γ, 𝑚). Let 𝑄(Γ) =PF(Γ) ∖ {𝐹 }.
The canonical condition is
𝑊 − 𝜑 ∈ Γ (𝜑 ∈ 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ)).
Every factorization of an element of 𝒜 contains no copy of 𝑚, so 𝒜 ⊆ 𝐻 . Moreover 𝑊 ∈ 𝒜 , and for
every 𝑤 ∈ 𝒜 one has 𝑤 − 𝑚 ≤ 𝐹, hence 𝑤 ≤ 𝑊.
2.8 From the PF condition to the condition for every gap
Let x be a nonnegative gap. Choose the largest integer 𝜑 in the finite set (𝑥 + Γ) ∖ Γ. For every positive
𝑠 ∈ Γ, maximality gives 𝜑 + 𝑠 ∈ Γ. Thus 𝜑 ∈ 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ), and 𝜑 − 𝑥 ∈ Γ.
𝑊 − 𝑥 = (𝑊 − 𝜑) + (𝜑 − 𝑥) ∈ Γ.
This direction is therefore obtained directly, without invoking an external positioned-semigroup criterion.
5

PAGE 7

2.9 Exact two-layer decomposition
For 𝑤 ∈ 𝒜 , set 𝑏 = 𝑊 − 𝑤 ≥ 0 . If 𝑤 = 0, then 𝑏 = 𝑊 ∈ 𝒜 . If 𝑤 > 0, then 𝑥 = 𝑤 − 𝑚 is a nonnegative
gap, so the preceding subsection gives 𝑏 + 𝑚 = 𝑊 − 𝑥 ∈ Γ.
• If 𝑏 ∈ Γ, then 𝑏 − 𝑚 ∈ Γ would imply 𝑤 + (𝑏 − 𝑚) = 𝐹 ∈ Γ, so 𝑏 ∈ 𝒜. In this case 𝑏 + 𝑚 ∉ 𝒜.
• If 𝑏 ∉ Γ, then 𝑏 + 𝑚 ∈ Γ and (𝑏 + 𝑚) − 𝑚 = 𝑏 ∉ Γ, so 𝑏 + 𝑚 ∈ 𝒜.
Thus exactly one of the following holds: 𝑊 − 𝑤 ∈ 𝒜 or 𝑊 + 𝑚 − 𝑤 ∈ 𝒜.
𝒜0 = {𝑤 ∈ 𝒜 ∶ 𝑊 − 𝑤 ∈ 𝒜}, 𝒜 1 = 𝒜 ∖ 𝒜 0.
If 𝑥 ≤Γ 𝑦 and 𝑦 ∈ 𝒜 0, then 𝑊 − 𝑥 = (𝑊 − 𝑦) + (𝑦 − 𝑥) ∈ Γ. Since its sum with the Apéry element 𝑥
is 𝑊, one has 𝑊 − 𝑥 ∈ 𝒜 . Hence 𝒜0 is a lower ideal and 𝒜1 an upper filter.
On 𝒜1, the map 𝜄(𝑤) = 𝑊 + 𝑚 − 𝑤again takes values in 𝒜1; moreover 𝜄2 = id and 𝜄(𝑥) − 𝜄(𝑦) = 𝑦 − 𝑥.
Thus 𝜄 is an order-reversing involution.
The maximal elements of the Apéry set are 𝑃 𝐹 + 𝑚. Indeed, if 𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 𝐹 , then 𝑞 + 𝑚 ∈ 𝒜 ; if a larger
Apéry element differed from it by 𝑠 > 0 in Γ, then 𝑞 + 𝑠 ∈ Γ , a contradiction. Conversely, if 𝑤 ∈ 𝒜 is
maximal, then 𝑞 = 𝑤 − 𝑚is a gap, and if 𝑞 + 𝑠were a gap for some positive 𝑠 ∈ Γ, then 𝑤 + 𝑠 ∈ 𝒜, contrary
to maximality.
If 𝑞 ∈ 𝑄 , then 𝑞 < 𝐹 . The inclusion 𝑞 + 𝑚 ∈ 𝒜 0 would give 𝑊 − (𝑞 + 𝑚) = 𝐹 − 𝑞 ∈ Γ ∖ {0},
contradicting maximality. Together with the fact that 𝒜1 is an upper filter, this yields
Max 𝒜1 = (𝑃 𝐹 ∖ {𝐹 }) + 𝑚,
Min 𝒜1 = {𝑊 − 𝑞 ∶ 𝑞 ∈ 𝑄}, | Min 𝒜1| = |𝑄| = 𝑡 − 1.
Moreover, 𝒜0 = {ℎ ∈ 𝐻 ∶ 𝑊 − ℎ ∈ 𝐻} . For an element ℎ on the right, if ℎ − 𝑚 ∈ Γ then 𝐹 =
(ℎ − 𝑚) + (𝑊 − ℎ) ∈ Γ, so ℎ ∈ 𝒜.
2.10 KEY : direct proof of the support-zero return
Let 𝑞 ∈ 𝑄 and 𝑐 = 𝑊 − 𝑞 ∈ Γ , and choose a direction 𝑖 for which 𝑐 − 𝑛𝑖 ∈ 𝐻 . The pseudo-Frobenius
property gives 𝑞 + 𝑛𝑖 ∈ Γ.
If 𝑞 + 𝑛𝑖 − 𝑚 ∈ Γ, then
𝐹 = (𝑐 − 𝑛𝑖) + (𝑞 + 𝑛𝑖 − 𝑚) ∈ Γ,
a contradiction. Therefore
𝑞 + 𝑛𝑖 ∈ 𝒜 ⊂ 𝐻.
If a genuine 𝐻-factorization of this element had positive 𝑛𝑖-coefficient, removing one copy of 𝑛𝑖 from that
same factorization would put 𝑞 in Γ. Hence
𝑐 − 𝑛𝑖 ∈ 𝐻 ⟹ 𝑞 + 𝑛 𝑖 ∈ ⟨𝑛𝑗, 𝑛𝑘⟩.
Since 𝑐 = 𝑊 − 𝑞 > 0 lies in 𝐻, the set 𝐷(𝑐)is nonempty. Thus, with the conventions 𝐷(𝑐) = {𝑖 ∶
𝑐 − 𝑛𝑖 ∈ 𝐻} and 𝑆𝐻(𝑞) = {𝑖 ∶ 𝑞 + 𝑛𝑖 ∈ 𝐻}, one has ∅ ≠ 𝐷(𝑐) ⊆ 𝑆𝐻(𝑞). Equality is not asserted. No
Hall-type existence theorem, factorization uniqueness, or generic connectivity is needed.
Furthermore, 𝑐 − 𝑚 = 𝐹 − 𝑞 is positive and does not lie in Γ, so 𝑐 ∈ 𝒜 . Also 𝑥 = 𝑐 − 𝑛 𝑖 ∈ 𝒜 , since
𝑥 − 𝑚 ∈ Γ would imply 𝑐 − 𝑚 ∈ Γ. With 𝑦 = 𝑞 + 𝑛𝑖 and ℎ = 𝑞 + 𝑚,
ℎ, 𝑐, 𝑥, 𝑦 ∈ 𝐻 ∩ 𝒜, ℎ + 𝑐 = 𝑊 + 𝑚, 𝑥 + 𝑦 = 𝑊 .

This conclusion is obtained separately for each supported direction. The classification of rows is carried
out in G4.
2.11 Tail gcd firewall
Assume 𝑄 ≠ ∅, and choose 𝑞 ∈ 𝑄. Then 𝑞 + 𝑚, 𝑊 , 𝑐 = 𝑊 − 𝑞all lie in 𝒜 ⊆ 𝐻 . Hence
𝑚 = (𝑞 + 𝑚) + (𝑊 − 𝑞) − 𝑊 ∈ ℤ𝐻.
Let 𝑑 = gcd(𝑛1, 𝑛2, 𝑛3). Then 𝑑 also divides 𝑚. Since Γ is a numerical semigroup, gcd (𝑚, 𝑛1, 𝑛2, 𝑛3) =
1, and therefore 𝑑 = 1.
The signed equality above is used only in the group ℤ𝐻; it is not called a nonnegative factorization.
2.12 Selection of four rows and minimality of the tail
Under the contradiction hypothesis, we may choose four distinct elements 𝑞1, 𝑞2, 𝑞3, 𝑞4 ∈ 𝑄(Γ). The Apéry
and KEY statements above hold for all of 𝑄, hence simultaneously for these four selected rows. If one tail
generator were a nonnegative combination of the other two, that generator would also be redundant in Γ,
contradicting edim Γ = 4 . Thus the tail is minimally generated. By § 2.11, its gcd is one. Hence 𝐻 is a
genuine minimally three-generated numerical semigroup, and it is either symmetric or nonsymmetric.
3 The symmetric three-generated tail
We treat the case in which the minimally three-generated tail obtained in the previous section is symmet-
ric. The main text establishes the correspondence between a stable ideal and the Apéry set, and Appendix
A excludes the possibility that all four candidates survive by a complete case analysis. The two-generated
semigroup 𝑇 = ⟨𝑢, 𝑣⟩ and the coefficient notation in this section are local to this section and Appendix A.
3.1 Exclusion of the symmetric tail
Let Γ = ⟨𝑚, 𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑧⟩be a minimally four-generated numerical semigroup, with multiplicity 𝑚. Assume that
𝐻 = ⟨𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑧⟩is symmetric and that
𝑚 + 𝐹 (Γ) − 𝑞 ∈ Γ (𝑞 ∈ 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ)) (CAN)
Then 𝑡(Γ) ≤ 4.
From now on write 𝐹 = 𝐹 (Γ), 𝑓 = 𝐹 (𝐻), and 𝑄 = 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ) ∖ {𝐹 }. A minimal generator of an ideal of a
semigroup means an element from which no positive semigroup element can be subtracted while remaining
in the ideal.
3.2 Stable core and the exact bridge
𝐾 = {𝑎 ∈ 𝐻 ∶ 𝑎 + 𝑛𝑚 ∈ 𝐻 (𝑛 ≥ 0)}, ℎ = min 𝐾.
By symmetry and Γ = 𝐻 + ℕ𝑚, for every integer 𝑡,
𝑡 ∉ Γ ⟺ 𝑓 − 𝑡 ∈ 𝐾. (GAP-K)
Hence 𝐹 = 𝑓 − ℎ . Since 0 ∉ 𝐾 (because 𝑚 ∉ 𝐻), we have ℎ ≥ min(𝐻 ∖ {0}) > 𝑚. Put
𝑟 = ℎ − 𝑚 = 𝑓 − 𝐹 − 𝑚 > 0, 𝑟 + 𝑛𝑚 ∈ 𝐻 (𝑛 ≥ 1). (FS)
7

PAGE 9
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
The set 𝐾 is a Γ-ideal, and GAP-K shows that 𝑞 ↦ 𝑓 − 𝑞 is a bijection from 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ)onto MinΓ 𝐾. Applying
CAN to these minimal generators gives
𝐽 ∶= 𝐾 − 𝑟 ⊆ Γ, min 𝐽 = 𝑚, 𝑚 ∈ 𝐽 .
Set
𝐼𝑟 = {𝑐 ∈ 𝐻 ∶ 𝑐 + 𝑟 ∈ 𝐻}
By FS, 𝑐 ∈ 𝐼 𝑟 ⇒ 𝑐 + 𝑟 ∈ 𝐾 , and therefore 𝐽 ∩ 𝐻 = 𝐼 𝑟.
We now prove directly that
MinΓ 𝐽 = {𝑚} ⊔ (Min𝐻 𝐼𝑟 ∩ Ap(Γ, 𝑚)). (BRIDGE)
Let 𝑗 ∈ MinΓ 𝐽 ∖ {𝑚}. If 𝑗 − 𝑚 ∈ Γ, then 𝑗 = 𝑚 + (𝑗 − 𝑚), contrary to 𝑚 ∈ 𝐽 and the minimality of 𝑗.
Hence 𝑗 lies in the Apéry set, and its factorizations contain no 𝑚, so 𝑗 ∈ 𝐻 . Minimality in 𝐻 follows from
minimality in Γ.
Conversely, let 𝑐 ∈ Min𝐻 𝐼𝑟 ∩ Ap. Since 𝐽 ⊆ Γ , one has 𝑐 − 𝑚 ∉ 𝐽. If 𝑐 − 𝑛 ∈ 𝐽 for some 𝑛 ∈ {𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑧},
then 𝑐 − 𝑛 ∈ Γ and 𝑐 − 𝑛 − 𝑚 ∉ Γ, so 𝑐 − 𝑛 ∈ 𝐻, hence 𝑐 − 𝑛 ∈ 𝐽 ∩ 𝐻 = 𝐼 𝑟. This contradicts minimality
in 𝐻, proving the reverse inclusion.
Thus
𝑡(Γ) = 1 + |Min𝐻 𝐼𝑟 ∩ Ap |. (TYPE-BRIDGE)
We now assume 𝑡 ≥ 5 and derive a contradiction.
3.3 Exact classification of RA W4
By the standard complete-intersection classification, after permuting the generators we may write
𝑥 = 𝑑𝑢, 𝑦 = 𝑑𝑣, 𝑧 = 𝑤, 𝐻 = ⟨𝑑𝑢, 𝑑𝑣, 𝑤⟩, gcd(𝑢, 𝑣) =gcd(𝑑, 𝑤) = 1, 𝑤 ∈ 𝑇 .
Since 𝐻 is minimally three-generated, 𝑑, 𝑢, 𝑣 ≥ 2. Every integer has a unique expression 𝑗𝑤 + 𝑑𝑡, with
0 ≤ 𝑗 < 𝑑 , and membership in 𝐻 is equivalent to 𝑡 ∈ 𝑇 . Consequently
𝑓 = (𝑑 − 1)𝑤 + 𝑑𝐹𝑇 .
Write 𝑟 = 𝑠𝑤 + 𝑑𝜌, with 0 ≤ 𝑠 < 𝑑 . If 𝑠 = 0, the raw minima of 𝐼𝑟 occur only in the layer 𝑗 = 0 , and the
two-generated lemma below gives at most two of them. Henceforth assume 1 ≤ 𝑠 ≤ 𝑑 − 1 . The only layers
containing raw minima of 𝐼𝑟 are 𝑗 = 0 and 𝑗 = 𝑑 − 𝑠, coming respectively from the 𝑇-minima of
𝐽0 = {𝑡 ∈ 𝑇 ∶ 𝑡 + 𝜌 ∈ 𝑇 }, 𝐽 1 = {𝑡 ∈ 𝑇 ∶ 𝑡 + 𝜌 + 𝑤 ∈ 𝑇 }
Every other layer allows one copy of 𝑤 to be subtracted.
For every 𝜂 = 𝑝𝑢 + 𝑞𝑣 , 0 ≤ 𝑞 < 𝑢 , the set {𝑡 ∈ 𝑇 ∶ 𝑡 + 𝜂 ∈ 𝑇 } has at most two generators. Indeed,
writing 𝑡 = 𝑎𝑢 + 𝑏𝑣, 0 ≤ 𝑏 < 𝑢, the required threshold for 𝑎 takes only the two values
max(0, −𝑝) (𝑏 < 𝑢 − 𝑞), max(0, −𝑝 − 𝑣) (𝑏 ≥ 𝑢 − 𝑞)
The two minima are distinct and incomparable exactly when 0 < 𝑞 < 𝑢, −𝑣 < 𝑝 < 0. In that case, putting
𝐴 = −𝑝, 𝐶 = 𝑞, 𝐵 = 𝑢 − 𝑞, 𝐷 = 𝑣 + 𝑝, 𝐴 + 𝐷 = 𝑣, 𝐵 + 𝐶 = 𝑢,
the minima are 𝐴𝑢and 𝐵𝑣, and their images are 𝐶𝑣 and 𝐷𝑢.
Hence | Min𝐻 𝐼𝑟| ≤ 4. Since 𝑡 ≥ 5, TYPE-BRIDGE implies that all four minima exist and all four remain
in the Apéry set. With positive integers 𝐴, 𝐵, 𝐶, 𝐷and their primed versions, the four pairs are
(𝑐𝑥, 𝑎𝑥) = (𝐴𝑥, 𝑠𝑤 + 𝐶𝑦), (𝑐𝑦, 𝑎𝑦) = (𝐵𝑦, 𝑠𝑤 + 𝐷𝑥),
(𝑐′
𝑥, 𝑎′
𝑥) = ((𝑑 − 𝑠)𝑤 + 𝐴′𝑥, 𝐶′𝑦), (𝑐′
𝑦, 𝑎′
𝑦) = ((𝑑 − 𝑠)𝑤 + 𝐵′𝑦, 𝐷′𝑥), (RA W4)
where in every case 𝑎 = 𝑐 + 𝑟, 𝐴 + 𝐷 = 𝐴′ + 𝐷′ = 𝑣, and 𝐵 + 𝐶 = 𝐵′ + 𝐶′ = 𝑢. Moreover
𝜌 = 𝑢𝑣 − 𝐴𝑢 − 𝐵𝑣 = −𝐴𝑢 + 𝐶𝑣 = −𝐵𝑣 + 𝐷𝑢.

3.4 Cross-layer rigidity and actual pseudo-Frobenius input
If 𝐴′ ≥ 𝐴 , then 𝑐′
𝑥 − 𝑐𝑥 = (𝑑 − 𝑠)𝑤 + (𝐴′ − 𝐴)𝑥 ∈ 𝐻 , contradicting raw incomparability. Hence
𝛼 ∶= 𝐴 − 𝐴′ > 0. Similarly 𝛽 ∶= 𝐵 − 𝐵′ > 0. Subtracting the threshold formulas for the two layers gives
𝑤 = 𝛼𝑢 + 𝛽𝑣, 𝐴 ′ = 𝐴 − 𝛼 > 0, 𝐵 ′ = 𝐵 − 𝛽 > 0,
𝐶′ = 𝐶 + 𝛽, 𝐷 ′ = 𝐷 + 𝛼. (CROSS-LAYER)
By BRIDGE, the four raw rows correspond to actual pseudo-Frobenius rows 𝑞 = 𝑓 − 𝑎. In particular,
𝑐𝑥 − 𝑚, 𝑐𝑦 − 𝑚, 𝑐′
𝑥 − 𝑚, 𝑐′
𝑦 − 𝑚 ∉ Γ. (ALL-AP)
If 𝑎𝑥 − 𝑚 ∈ 𝐻, then 𝑎𝑥 ∈ 𝐾 implies 𝑎𝑥 − 𝑚 ∈ 𝐾; applying CAN would then give 𝑐𝑥 − 𝑚 ∈ Γ, impossible.
The same argument applies on the 𝑦-side. By symmetry of 𝐻,
𝑞𝑥 + 𝑚, 𝑞𝑦 + 𝑚 ∈ 𝐻. (QM)
3.5 Complete division into Branch I and Branch II
Write 𝑚 = 𝑒𝑤 + 𝑑𝜇, with 0 ≤ 𝑒 < 𝑑 . Since 𝑚 ∉ 𝐻, 𝜇 ∉ 𝑇 . Put ℓ = 𝑑 − 1 − 𝑠 and
𝑘0 = ⌊ ℓ + 𝑒
𝑑 ⌋ ∈ {0, 1}, 𝜃 = 𝜇 + 𝑘 0𝑤
From the representation of 𝑓, 𝑞𝑥 = ℓ𝑤 + 𝑑((𝐵 − 1)𝑣 − 𝑢)and 𝑞𝑦 = ℓ𝑤 + 𝑑((𝐴 − 1)𝑢 − 𝑣). Thus QM
becomes
𝜃 + (𝐵 − 1)𝑣 − 𝑢 ∈ 𝑇 , 𝜃 + (𝐴 − 1)𝑢 − 𝑣 ∈ 𝑇 .
Applying 2GI to 𝑋 = 𝜃 + (𝐴 − 1)𝑢 + (𝐵 − 1)𝑣gives
𝜃 − 𝑢 − 𝑣 ∈ 𝑇 or 𝜃 − 𝜌 − 𝑢 − 𝑣 ∈ 𝑇 . (SPLIT)
We call the left condition Branch I and the right condition Branch II. Their possible overlap causes no diffi-
culty. We first exclude Branch I.
3.5.1 T wo-generated calculations and completion of the case analysis
The normal form and intersection formula for the two-generated semigroup used here are proved in § A.1 of
Appendix A. It remains to exclude the assumption that all four raw minima survive in the Apéry set. The two
conditions in SPLIT cover the entire range. Allowing overlap, contradictions are obtained in the following
order.

In Branch I, we first separate the case in which the return index is one. If both rectangle hits were later
returns, the first stable point would be a gap, so at least one of them must be the first return. Classifying that
point as X-only, Y -only, or XY, the first two possibilities lack the required 𝑈𝑥 or 𝑈𝑦 hit, respectively, while
in the XY case the second stable point is a gap. The key point in this branch is not to apply to the first return
a predecessor constraint that is valid only for later returns.

PAGE 11
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
For Branch II with 𝑘0 = 1, we first establish positivity of the auxiliary quantity and then move backward
along the two D-rows. Since both backward points are gaps, symmetry of the two-generated semigroup and
the intersection formula imply the Branch I condition. When 𝑘0 = 0, the case 𝑒 = 0 is excluded directly. For
𝑒 > 0, we distinguish whether a carry occurs. Either the complement representation lies in the semigroup or
the actual immediately preceding stable point is a gap; in the final step this contradicts the pseudo-Frobenius
property of the same 𝑞𝑥.
All of these calculations are given in Appendix A. Therefore the four raw minima cannot all remain in the
Apéry set, and the assertion for the symmetric tail follows from BRIDGE.
4 Nonsymmetric factorization geometry and classification into three termi-
nal configurations
Assume that the three-generated tail is nonsymmetric. We first classify the supported directions of a pseudo-
Frobenius element into six arms, then establish coexistence restrictions for pairs of rows and exclude three
arms of the same color. Finally, we exhaust the possibilities for four distinct rows selected from a putative
counterexample and obtain three terminal configurations with their exact coefficient ranges.
4.1 Common setting
Let Γ = ⟨𝑚, 𝑛𝑖, 𝑛𝑗, 𝑛𝑘⟩, let 𝑚 be the multiplicity, let 𝐹 = 𝐹 (Γ), 𝑊 = 𝐹 + 𝑚 , and 𝐻 = ⟨𝑛 𝑖, 𝑛𝑗, 𝑛𝑘⟩.
Canonical reduction gives
𝑞 ∈ 𝑄 ∶= 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ) ∖ {𝐹 } ⟹ 𝑐𝑞 = 𝑊 − 𝑞 ∈ Γ
For every actual 𝑞, we have 𝑞 + 𝑚, 𝑐𝑞 ∈ 𝐻, 𝑐𝑞 − 𝑚 ∉ Γ, and 𝑞 ∉ Γ, by the elementary canonical lemmas.
𝑆𝐻(𝑞) = {𝑟 ∶ 𝑞 + 𝑛𝑟 ∈ 𝐻}, 𝐷(𝑐) = {𝑟 ∶ 𝑐 − 𝑛 𝑟 ∈ 𝐻}.
KEY says ∅ ≠ 𝐷(𝑐𝑞) ⊆ 𝑆𝐻(𝑞); equality is not assumed. The KEY implication itself follows directly: if
𝑞 + 𝑛𝑟 − 𝑚 ∈ Γ, then 𝐹 = (𝑐𝑞 − 𝑛𝑟) + (𝑞 + 𝑛𝑟 − 𝑚) ∈ Γ, a contradiction.
For a cyclic order (i,j,k), we use the Herzog convention
𝜌𝑖 = 𝑎𝑖 + 𝑏𝑖, 𝜌 𝑖𝑛𝑖 = 𝑏𝑗𝑛𝑗 + 𝑎𝑘𝑛𝑘,
𝜌𝑗𝑛𝑗 = 𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘, 𝜌 𝑘𝑛𝑘 = 𝑏𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑎𝑗𝑛𝑗, (H)
where all 𝑎𝑟, 𝑏𝑟 are positive integers and 𝜌𝑟 is the critical multiplier of 𝑛𝑟 in 𝐻.
By KEY, 𝑆𝐻(𝑞)is nonempty. A row with 𝑆𝐻(𝑞) = {𝑟}is called a singleton 𝑆𝑟; a row with |𝑆𝐻(𝑞)| = 2
is called a doubleton; and a row with 𝑆𝐻(𝑞) = {𝑖, 𝑗, 𝑘}is called a corner. For a doubleton, the missing
direction 𝑟 is defined by 𝑟 ∉ 𝑆𝐻(𝑞). The six-arm atlas for doubletons is given in § 4.1.1:
𝐴𝑟(𝜆) = 𝑓𝐴 − 𝜆𝑛𝑟 (1 ≤ 𝜆 ≤ 𝑎𝑟 − 1),
𝐵𝑟(𝜇) = 𝑓𝐵 − 𝜇𝑛𝑟 (1 ≤ 𝜇 ≤ 𝑏𝑟 − 1).
The exact formulas for the pseudo-Frobenius corners, including all three cyclic representations, are
𝑓𝐴 = (𝜌𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑎𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 − 𝑛𝑘 = −𝑛𝑖 + (𝜌𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑎𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘,
𝑓𝐵 = (𝜌𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 − 𝑛𝑗 + (𝑏𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘, 𝑓 𝐴 − 𝑓𝐵 = 𝑎𝑗𝑛𝑗 − 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘. (PF)
T wo rows on the same arm, and a corner together with an arm of the same color, contradict the pseudo-
Frobenius antichain property. There is at most one lower corner with triple support. A higher corner would
give 𝐹 (𝐻) ≥ 𝐹 (Γ)while its corresponding 𝑞 must satisfy 𝑞 < 𝐹 (Γ), a contradiction.

PAGE 12
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
4.1.1 Short proof of the six-arm atlas
Assume 𝑞 ∉ 𝐻 , 𝑞 + 𝑛 𝑗, 𝑞 + 𝑛𝑘 ∈ 𝐻 , and 𝑞 + 𝑛 𝑖 ∉ 𝐻 . Choose the largest integer ℓ ≥ 0 such that
𝑞 + ℓ𝑛 𝑖 ∉ 𝐻 . It exists because 𝐻 is a numerical semigroup, and ℓ ≥ 1 because 𝑞 + 𝑛 𝑖 ∉ 𝐻 . Then
𝑓 = 𝑞 + ℓ𝑛 𝑖 satisfies 𝑓 + 𝑛𝑖 ∈ 𝐻; moreover, adding ℓ𝑛𝑖 to 𝑞 + 𝑛𝑗, 𝑞 + 𝑛𝑘 ∈ 𝐻 gives 𝑓 + 𝑛𝑗, 𝑓 + 𝑛𝑘 ∈ 𝐻.
Hence 𝑓 ∈ 𝑃 𝐹 (𝐻) = {𝑓𝐴, 𝑓𝐵}.
Suppose first that 𝑓 = 𝑓 𝐴 and ℓ ≥ 𝑎 𝑖. The canonical formula is
𝑞 + 𝑛𝑗 = (𝑎𝑖 − ℓ − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝜌𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘.
Comparing this with a genuine H-factorization 𝑋𝑛𝑖 + 𝑌 𝑛𝑗 + 𝑍𝑛𝑘 gives
(𝜌𝑘 − 1 − 𝑍)𝑛𝑘 = (ℓ − 𝑎𝑖 + 1 + 𝑋)𝑛𝑖 + 𝑌 𝑛𝑗.
The right-hand side is positive, so the coefficient on the left is positive and is strictly less than𝜌𝑘, contradicting
criticality. Thus 1 ≤ ℓ ≤ 𝑎 𝑖 − 1.
If 𝑓 = 𝑓 𝐵, the same argument, using 𝑞 + 𝑛𝑘 = (𝑏𝑖 − ℓ − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝜌𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗, gives ℓ ≤ 𝑏 𝑖 − 1.
Conversely, for 𝐴𝑖(𝜆), 1 ≤ 𝜆 ≤ 𝑎𝑖 − 1,
𝑞 + 𝑛𝑗 = (𝑎𝑖 − 𝜆 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝜌𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘 ∈ 𝐻,
𝑞 + 𝑛𝑘 = (𝜌𝑖 − 𝜆 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑎𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 ∈ 𝐻.
If either 𝑞 or 𝑞 + 𝑛 𝑖 belonged to 𝐻, adding the missing nonnegative multiple of 𝑛𝑖 would put 𝑓𝐴 in 𝐻,
impossible. Thus both are gaps. The B-case follows from the corresponding two canonical formulas. The
color and the depth are uniquely determined by the maximal ℓ above.
This proves the six-arm atlas from 𝑆𝑇 𝐷𝐻𝐸𝑅𝑍𝑂𝐺.
4.2 Basic actual antichain and coefficient constraints
For distinct pseudo-Frobenius rows 𝑞, 𝑞′, neither 𝑞 − 𝑞′ nor 𝑞′ − 𝑞 lies in Γ. Hence distinct complements 𝑐, 𝑐′
also form a Γ-antichain.
For a singleton 𝑆𝑟, KEY implies 𝑐𝑆 = 𝜅 𝑟𝑛𝑟. If some H-factorization of 𝑐𝑆 used another direction, then
𝐷(𝑐𝑆) ⊆ {𝑟}would fail. If 𝜅𝑟 ≥ 𝜌𝑟, relation (H) would provide such a factorization in the other directions.
Therefore
1 ≤ 𝜅𝑟 ≤ 𝜌𝑟 − 1. (SR)
There cannot be two singletons in the same direction, by comparability along the pure ray. If 𝑆𝑟 exists,
then in every H-factorization of every other complement the 𝑟-coordinate is at most 𝜅𝑟 − 1. Otherwise
𝑐 − 𝜅𝑟𝑛𝑟 ∈ 𝐻 , contradicting the complement antichain; if the difference were zero, the two rows would
coincide.
Likewise, every H-factorization of 𝑊 has 𝑟-coordinate at most 𝜅𝑟 − 1: if it contained 𝜅𝑟 copies, removing
them would put 𝑞𝑆 in 𝐻. Since 𝑞𝑆 + 𝑛𝑟 ∈ 𝐻, the maximum 𝜅𝑟 − 1 is attained.
The complement of an arm missing direction 𝑖 uses no copy of 𝑛𝑖 and has the form
𝑐 = 𝑦𝑛𝑗 + 𝑧𝑛𝑘, 0 ≤ 𝑦 < 𝜌 𝑗, 0 ≤ 𝑧 < 𝜌 𝑘. (CB)
Indeed, if 𝑦 ≥ 𝜌𝑗 or 𝑧 ≥ 𝜌𝑘, relation (H) produces a representation involving 𝑛𝑖, contradicting KEY . This
two-coefficient representation is unique: the difference of two such representations would be a pure relation
between 𝑛𝑗 and 𝑛𝑘, and any nonzero such relation would place a critical multiplier below 𝜌𝑗 or 𝜌𝑘 on one
side.
11

PAGE 13
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
4.3 Critical-box uniqueness and an integer kernel basis
Critical-box uniqueness. There do not exist two distinct nonnegative factorizations for which every co-
ordinate satisfies 𝑋𝑟 < 𝜌 𝑟. Their difference would be a nonzero kernel vector. With three coordinates,
one sign class consists of a single coordinate, and its absolute value is strictly below the corresponding 𝜌𝑟,
contradicting criticality.
Integer basis.
𝑟𝑗 = (𝑎𝑖, −𝜌𝑗, 𝑏𝑘), 𝑟 𝑘 = (𝑏𝑖, 𝑎𝑗, −𝜌𝑘)
form a basis of the integer kernel. They are linearly independent and span the real kernel. Given any integer
kernel vector, subtract the floor multiples of its real coefficients to obtain
𝑣 = 𝑥𝑟𝑗 + 𝑦𝑟𝑘 ∈ ℤ 3, 0 ≤ 𝑥, 𝑦 < 1.
If 𝑥 + 𝑦 > 0, then 0 < 𝑣𝑖 < 𝜌𝑖, −𝜌𝑗 < 𝑣𝑗 < 𝑎𝑗, and −𝜌𝑘 < 𝑣𝑘 < 𝑏𝑘. If 𝑣𝑗, 𝑣𝑘 ≤ 0, criticality in direction
𝑖 gives a contradiction. If 𝑣𝑗 ≥ 0 , the negative 𝑘-coordinate has absolute value below 𝜌𝑘, contradicting
𝑘-criticality. If 𝑣𝑘 ≥ 0 , the same argument uses 𝑗-criticality. A nonzero all-nonnegative kernel vector is
impossible because all 𝑛𝑟 are positive. Therefore 𝑥 = 𝑦 = 0 , and the original real coefficients were integers.
This lemma uses no hidden connectivity or global factorization uniqueness. Whenever uniqueness is in-
voked below, it is only the critical-box uniqueness stated explicitly above.
4.4 Corners and singletons cannot coexist
Assume that the corner 𝑓𝐴 and the singleton 𝑆𝑗 coexist. Let 𝑐0 = 𝑊 − 𝑓 𝐴 ∈ 𝐻 and 𝑐𝑆 = 𝜅𝑛 𝑗, with
𝜅 ≤ 𝜌𝑗 − 1. For the same actual 𝑞𝑆𝑗
,
𝑞𝑆 + 𝑛𝑖 = 𝑓𝐴 + 𝑐0 − 𝜅𝑛𝑗 + 𝑛𝑖 = (𝜌𝑗 − 1 − 𝜅)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑎𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘 + 𝑐0 ∈ 𝐻.
Direction 𝑖 is missing for 𝑆𝑗, a contradiction. Cyclic permutation treats every singleton direction, and the
case 𝑓𝐵 follows by color reversal.
This is a stronger local coexistence exclusion than merely excluding corners among four selected rows.
A corner with three arms of the opposite color and no singleton still requires the separate COLOR-CAP
argument below.
4.5 Synchronization of a matched 𝐴𝑖/𝐵𝑖 pair
Take actual rows 𝐴𝑖(𝜆), 𝐵𝑖(𝜇)with complements
𝑐𝐴 = 𝑦𝑛𝑗 + 𝑧𝑛𝑘, 𝑐 𝐵 = 𝑦′𝑛𝑗 + 𝑧′𝑛𝑘
They satisfy the bounds in (CB). Let Δ𝑗 = 𝑦′ − 𝑦 and Δ𝑘 = 𝑧′ − 𝑧. Then
𝑣 = (𝜇 − 𝜆, 𝑎𝑗 − Δ𝑗, −𝑏𝑘 − Δ𝑘) ∈kerℤ(𝑛).
By §4.3, write 𝑣 = 𝑢𝑟𝑗 + 𝑣0𝑟𝑘, with 𝑢, 𝑣0 ∈ ℤ. The coordinate bounds are
2 − 𝑎𝑖 ≤ 𝑣𝑖 ≤ 𝑏𝑖 − 2,
1 − 𝑏𝑗 ≤ 𝑣𝑗 ≤ 𝜌𝑗 + 𝑎𝑗 − 1, 𝑣 𝑘 ≤ 𝑎𝑘 − 1.
If 𝑢 ≥ 1, 𝑣 0 ≤ 0 , then the 𝑗-coordinate is at most −𝜌𝑗, impossible. If 𝑢 ≤ −1, 𝑣 0 ≥ 1 , then the 𝑗-
coordinate is at least 𝜌𝑗 + 𝑎𝑗, impossible. If 𝑢 ≥ 0, 𝑣0 ≥ 1, then the 𝑖-coordinate is at least 𝑏𝑖, impossible. If
12

PAGE 14
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
𝑢 ≤ −1, 𝑣0 ≤ 0, then the 𝑖-coordinate is at most −𝑎𝑖, impossible. The remaining case 𝑢 = 0, 𝑣0 ≤ −1 gives
𝑘 ≥ 𝜌𝑘, also impossible. Thus 𝑢 = 𝑣0 = 0.
Therefore
𝜆 = 𝜇, 𝑦 ′ = 𝑦 + 𝑎𝑗, 𝑧 ′ = 𝑧 − 𝑏𝑘.
Putting 𝑔 = 𝑦 ≥ 0 and 𝛼 = 𝑧′ ≥ 0, we obtain
𝑃 = 𝜌 𝑖 − 𝜆, 𝑅 = 𝑎 𝑗 + 𝑔 < 𝜌𝑗, 𝑇 = 𝑏 𝑘 + 𝛼 < 𝜌𝑘,
𝑐𝐴 = 𝑔𝑛𝑗 + 𝑇 𝑛𝑘, 𝑐 𝐵 = 𝑅𝑛𝑗 + 𝛼𝑛𝑘,
𝑊 = (𝑃 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 1)𝑛𝑘. (MATCH)
Moreover 𝑃 > 𝑎 𝑖, 𝑏𝑖, 𝑔 ≤ 𝑏 𝑗 − 1, and 𝛼 ≤ 𝑎 𝑘 − 1. We do not assume strict positivity of 𝑔 or 𝛼; the
boundary cases 𝑔 = 0 and 𝛼 = 0 are included.
4.5.1 Matched pair and singleton
If 𝑆𝑗 exists, then 𝜅𝑗 < 𝜌𝑗. The complement antichain and 𝑐𝐵 = 𝑅𝑛𝑗 + 𝛼𝑛𝑘 imply 𝜅𝑗 > 𝑅 (if equality holds
and 𝛼 = 0, the complements coincide; otherwise they are comparable). For the same 𝑞𝑆𝑗
,
𝑞𝑆𝑗
+ 𝑛𝑘 = (𝑃 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 𝜅𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + 𝑇 𝑛𝑘
Using 𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘 = 𝜌𝑗𝑛𝑗 once gives
𝑞𝑆𝑗
+ 𝑛𝑘 = (𝑃 − 𝑎𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 𝜅𝑗 − 1 + 𝜌𝑗)𝑛𝑗 + 𝛼𝑛𝑘 ∈ 𝐻.
Since 𝑃 − 𝑎𝑖 − 1 = 𝑏 𝑖 − 𝜆 − 1 ≥ 0 and 𝜅𝑗 ≤ 𝜌𝑗 − 1, all coefficients are nonnegative, contradicting the
missing direction 𝑘.
The case 𝑆𝑘 is dual: 𝜅𝑘 > 𝑇 , and replacing 𝑏𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑎𝑗𝑛𝑗 by 𝜌𝑘𝑛𝑘 in 𝑞𝑆𝑘
+ 𝑛𝑗 again gives a nonnegative
H-representation. Thus the only singleton that could coexist with a matched pair at this stage is 𝑆𝑖.
4.6 Root-free matched-pair level rigidity
Fix the same actual matched pair of § 4.5, with 𝑞𝐴 = 𝐴𝑖(𝜆), 𝑞𝐵 = 𝐵𝑖(𝜆), 𝛿 = 𝑎𝑖 − 𝜆 ≥ 1, 𝛽 = 𝑏𝑖 − 𝜆 ≥ 1,
and 𝑔, 𝛼 ≥ 0. Let 𝑃 = 𝜌 𝑖 − 𝜆, 𝑅 = 𝑎𝑗 + 𝑔, 𝑇 = 𝑏𝑘 + 𝛼. Then there exist a positive integer 𝐿 and nonnegative
integers 𝑡, 𝑢such that
𝑞𝐴 + 𝑛𝑖 = 𝐿𝑚 + (𝑎𝑗 + 𝑡)𝑛𝑗 + 𝑢𝑛𝑘, 𝑞 𝐵 + 𝑛𝑖 = 𝐿𝑚 + 𝑡𝑛𝑗 + (𝑢 + 𝑏𝑘)𝑛𝑘,
𝑃 𝑛𝑖 = 𝐿𝑚 + (𝑡 + 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑢 + 1)𝑛𝑘.
The proof retains the cases 𝑔 = 0 and 𝛼 = 0. Below, we refer to the complement representations in § 4.5 as
COMP .
4.7 Actual positive-m returns can be chosen independently
By the pseudo-Frobenius property, 𝐸𝐴 = 𝑞 𝐴 + 𝑛𝑖 and 𝐸𝐵 = 𝑞 𝐵 + 𝑛𝑖 belong to Γ. No Γ-factorization of
either element can contain 𝑛𝑖, since removing one copy of 𝑛𝑖 from that same nonnegative coefficient vector
would put 𝑞𝐴 or 𝑞𝐵 in Γ.
Moreover, neither 𝐸𝐴 nor 𝐸𝐵 lies in 𝐻. We give the short pure-H proofs separately.
13

PAGE 15
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
4.7.1 𝐸𝐴 ∉ 𝐻
Suppose 𝐸𝐴 = 𝑋𝑛𝑗 + 𝑌 𝑛𝑘, with 𝑋, 𝑌 ≥ 0 . The canonical formula gives
𝑃 𝑛𝑖 = (𝑋 − 𝑎𝑗 + 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑌 + 1)𝑛𝑘.
If 𝑋 ≥ 𝑎 𝑗 − 1, this contradicts criticality because 0 < 𝑃 < 𝜌 𝑖.
If 𝑋 ≤ 𝑎 𝑗 − 2, then
(𝑌 + 1)𝑛𝑘 = 𝑃 𝑛𝑖 + (𝑎𝑗 − 1 − 𝑋)𝑛𝑗.
Criticality gives 𝑌 + 1 ≥ 𝜌 𝑘. Subtracting 𝑅𝑘 once yields
𝛿𝑛𝑖 = (𝑋 + 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑌 + 1 − 𝜌𝑘)𝑛𝑘.
This contradicts 0 < 𝛿 < 𝜌 𝑖. The coefficient of 𝑚 has been zero throughout; hence the applications of
criticality are legitimate.
4.7.2 𝐸𝐵 ∉ 𝐻
Similarly, if 𝐸𝐵 = 𝑋𝑛𝑗 + 𝑌 𝑛𝑘, then
𝑃 𝑛𝑖 = (𝑋 + 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑌 − 𝑏𝑘 + 1)𝑛𝑘.
If 𝑌 ≥ 𝑏 𝑘 − 1, then 𝑃 < 𝜌 𝑖 contradicts criticality. If 𝑌 ≤ 𝑏 𝑘 − 2, then
(𝑋 + 1)𝑛𝑗 = 𝑃 𝑛𝑖 + (𝑏𝑘 − 1 − 𝑌 )𝑛𝑘,
so 𝑋 + 1 ≥ 𝜌 𝑗. Subtracting 𝑅𝑗 gives
𝛽𝑛𝑖 = (𝑋 + 1 − 𝜌𝑗)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑌 + 1)𝑛𝑘,
contradicting 0 < 𝛽 < 𝜌 𝑖.
Thus both actual elements admit positive-m returns. For each element independently, choose the least
positive coefficient of 𝑚 occurring in a factorization and fix one factorization that realizes it:
𝐸𝐴 = 𝐿𝐴𝑚 + 𝑈𝑗𝑛𝑗 + 𝑈𝑘𝑛𝑘, 𝐸 𝐵 = 𝐿𝐵𝑚 + 𝑉𝑗𝑛𝑗 + 𝑉𝑘𝑛𝑘, (RET)
𝐿𝐴, 𝐿𝐵 ≥ 1, 𝑈 𝑗, 𝑈𝑘, 𝑉𝑗, 𝑉𝑘 ≥ 0.
These are explicit minima of nonempty subsets of the positive integers. The semigroup, 𝐹 , 𝑊 , 𝑞𝐴, 𝑞𝐵, and
the canonical coefficients remain fixed; only the two nonnegative coefficient vectors in RET are chosen.
4.8 Four coordinate caps
Adding RET and COMP and removing one copy of 𝑚gives two genuine representations of the same element
𝐹 + 𝑛𝑖:
𝐹 + 𝑛𝑖 = (𝐿𝐴 − 1)𝑚 + (𝑈𝑗 + 𝑔)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑈𝑘 + 𝑇 )𝑛𝑘,
𝐹 + 𝑛𝑖 = (𝐿𝐵 − 1)𝑚 + (𝑉𝑗 + 𝑅)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑉𝑘 + 𝛼)𝑛𝑘 (FI)
For example, if 𝑈𝑗 + 𝑔 ≥ 𝜌 𝑗, replace the contained block 𝜌𝑗𝑛𝑗 by 𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘. Removing the resulting
copy of 𝑛𝑖 from the same final representation would give 𝐹 ∈ Γ ; the condition 𝑎𝑖 ≥ 1 supplies that copy.
The other three cases are analogous, using 𝑅𝑗 or 𝑅𝑘. Hence
14

PAGE 16
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
𝑈𝑗 + 𝑔 ≤ 𝜌𝑗 − 1, 𝑈 𝑘 + 𝑇 ≤ 𝜌 𝑘 − 1,
𝑉𝑗 + 𝑅 ≤ 𝜌𝑗 − 1, 𝑉 𝑘 + 𝛼 ≤ 𝜌𝑘 − 1. (CAP)
4.9 Uniform exclusion of unequal levels
Set
𝑡 = 𝑈𝑗 − 𝑎𝑗, 𝐾 = 𝑈 𝑘 + 𝑏𝑘, 𝑥 = 𝑉 𝑗 − 𝑡, 𝑦 = 𝐾 − 𝑉 𝑘.
Using the canonical difference 𝐸𝐵 = 𝐸𝐴 − 𝑎𝑗𝑛𝑗 + 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘 and RET gives
(𝐿𝐴 − 𝐿𝐵)𝑚 = 𝑥𝑛𝑗 − 𝑦𝑛𝑘. (DIFF)
CAP implies in particular
−𝜌𝑗 < 𝑥 < 𝜌 𝑗, −𝜌 𝑘 < 𝑦 < 𝜌 𝑘. (BOX)
More precisely,
𝑅 + 1 − 𝜌𝑗 ≤ 𝑥 ≤ 𝜌 𝑗 − 𝑔 − 1, 𝑇 + 1 − 𝜌 𝑘 ≤ 𝑦 ≤ 𝜌 𝑘 − 𝛼 − 1.
4.9.1 𝐿𝐴 > 𝐿𝐵 is impossible
Let 𝜎 = 𝐿 𝐴 − 𝐿𝐵 ≥ 1. If 𝑉𝑘 ≥ 𝑏𝑘, then
𝐸𝐴 = 𝐿𝐵𝑚 + (𝑉𝑗 + 𝑎𝑗)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑉𝑘 − 𝑏𝑘)𝑛𝑘
is an actual positive-m return for 𝐸𝐴 at a strictly smaller level, contradicting the minimality of 𝐿𝐴. Thus
𝑉𝑘 ≤ 𝑏𝑘 − 1.
Consequently 𝐶 ∶= 𝑦 = 𝑈 𝑘 + 𝑏𝑘 − 𝑉𝑘 ≥ 1, and DIFF becomes
𝜎𝑚 + 𝐶𝑛𝑘 = Δ𝑛𝑗, Δ ∶= 𝑥 > 0.
By BOX, Δ < 𝜌𝑗.
Adding 𝑅𝑗 as a completed zero identity yields the exact representation
𝑊 = (𝛽 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝜌𝑗 + 𝑅 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝛼 − 1)𝑛𝑘 (WJ)
of the same number 𝑊. If 𝛼 = 0, the 𝑘-coefficient is −1, so this is only a signed intermediate equality.
Add the completed identity 𝜎𝑚 + 𝐶𝑛𝑘 − Δ𝑛𝑗 = 0, and use 𝐹 = 𝑊 − 𝑚 . Then
𝐹 = (𝜎 − 1)𝑚 + (𝛽 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝜌𝑗 + 𝑅 − 1 − Δ)𝑛𝑗 + (𝛼 + 𝐶 − 1)𝑛𝑘 ∈ Γ. (ABS+)
Every coefficient in this final expression is nonnegative, a contradiction. In particular, when 𝛼 = 0 , the
inequality 𝐶 ≥ 1 gives 𝛼 + 𝐶 − 1 ≥ 0. No stronger bound such as 𝜎 ≥ 3 or 𝜎 ≥ 5 is needed.
15

PAGE 17
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
4.9.2 𝐿𝐵 > 𝐿𝐴 is impossible
Let 𝜎 = 𝐿 𝐵 − 𝐿𝐴 ≥ 1. If 𝑡 ≥ 0, then
𝐸𝐵 = 𝐿𝐴𝑚 + 𝑡𝑛𝑗 + 𝐾𝑛𝑘
is an actual positive-m return for 𝐸𝐵 at a lower level, contradicting the minimality of 𝐿𝐵. Hence 𝑡 < 0.
Thus Δ ∶= 𝑥 = 𝑉 𝑗 − 𝑡 ≥ 1, and DIFF becomes
𝜎𝑚 + Δ𝑛𝑗 = 𝐶𝑛𝑘, 𝐶 ∶= 𝑦 > 0.
By BOX, 𝐶 < 𝜌𝑘.
Adding 𝑅𝑘 as a completed zero identity yields the exact representation
𝑊 = (𝛿 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑔 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝜌𝑘 + 𝑇 − 1)𝑛𝑘 (WK)
of the same number 𝑊. If 𝑔 = 0, the 𝑗-coefficient is −1, so this is only a signed intermediate equality.
Adding 𝜎𝑚 + Δ𝑛𝑗 − 𝐶𝑛𝑘 = 0 and using 𝐹 = 𝑊 − 𝑚 gives
𝐹 = (𝜎 − 1)𝑚 + (𝛿 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑔 + Δ − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝜌𝑘 + 𝑇 − 𝐶 − 1)𝑛𝑘 ∈ Γ. (ABS-)
Again all coefficients are nonnegative. In particular, when 𝑔 = 0, Δ ≥ 1 gives 𝑔 + Δ − 1 ≥ 0. This is a
contradiction.
4.10 Equal-level matching and exclusion of PRE
It follows that 𝐿𝐴 = 𝐿𝐵 =∶ 𝐿. Then DIFF gives 𝑥𝑛𝑗 = 𝑦𝑛𝑘.
By BOX and tail criticality, 𝑥 = 𝑦 = 0 . Indeed, if one is zero then both are zero, while if they are nonzero
they have the same sign and |𝑥|𝑛𝑗 = |𝑦|𝑛𝑘 contradicts 0 < |𝑥| < 𝜌 𝑗.
Hence
𝑉𝑗 = 𝑡 ≥ 0, 𝑉 𝑘 = 𝑈𝑘 + 𝑏𝑘.
Putting 𝑢 ∶= 𝑈𝑘, we obtain
𝐸𝐴 = 𝐿𝑚 + (𝑎𝑗 + 𝑡)𝑛𝑗 + 𝑢𝑛𝑘, 𝐸 𝐵 = 𝐿𝑚 + 𝑡𝑛𝑗 + (𝑢 + 𝑏𝑘)𝑛𝑘, 𝑡, 𝑢 ≥ 0. (EA/EB)
Comparison with the canonical formula for 𝐸𝐴 gives
𝑃 𝑛𝑖 = 𝐿𝑚 + (𝑡 + 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑢 + 1)𝑛𝑘. (P-FIBER)
The right-hand side is genuine. Thus the PRE region 𝑡 < 0 has been excluded completely.
4.10.1 Matched pair and extra arms
The root-free level theorem § 4.6, using only (MATCH) and the two actual pseudo-Frobenius rows, gives
𝑞𝐴 + 𝑛𝑖 = 𝐿𝑚 + (𝑎𝑗 + 𝑡)𝑛𝑗 + 𝑢𝑛𝑘,
𝑞𝐵 + 𝑛𝑖 = 𝐿𝑚 + 𝑡𝑛𝑗 + (𝑢 + 𝑏𝑘)𝑛𝑘, 𝐿 ≥ 1, 𝑡, 𝑢 ≥ 0 (EA/EB)
No unit-root hypothesis is used in this synchronization.
16

PAGE 18
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
Therefore the genuine factorization 𝑓𝐴 = (𝑞𝐴 + 𝑛𝑖) + (𝜆 − 1)𝑛𝑖 has 𝑗-coordinate at least 𝑎𝑗. If an actual
𝐴𝑗(𝜇), with 𝜇 ≤ 𝑎𝑗 − 1, were present, then removing 𝜇𝑛𝑗 from this same factorization would put 𝐴𝑗 in Γ, a
contradiction. Similarly, the 𝑘-coordinate of 𝑓𝐵 is at least 𝑏𝑘, so 𝐵𝑘 cannot coexist.
Thus the only additional arms compatible with a matched pair in direction 𝑖 are 𝐵𝑗 and 𝐴𝑘. We use this
coexistence restriction in the classification below.
4.10.2 A matched pair cannot coexist with 𝑆𝑖 either
From (EA/EB),
𝑃 𝑛𝑖 = 𝐿𝑚 + (𝑡 + 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑢 + 1)𝑛𝑘
so 𝑃 𝑛𝑖 − 𝑚 ∈ Γ . If the complement of the singleton 𝑆𝑖 is 𝜅𝑛𝑖, then the genuine representation (MATCH)
of 𝑊 shows that 𝜅 ≤ 𝑃 − 1 would imply 𝑞𝑆𝑖
∈ 𝐻. Hence 𝜅 ≥ 𝑃 . It follows that
𝑐𝑆𝑖
− 𝑚 = (𝜅 − 𝑃 )𝑛𝑖 + (𝑃 𝑛𝑖 − 𝑚) ∈ Γ,
contradicting the Apéry property of the actual complement. Thus a matched pair cannot coexist with any
singleton. This eliminates all configurations containing both a matched pair and a singleton.
4.11 Exact complement geometry for two arms of the same color
Take actual rows 𝐴𝑖(𝜆), 𝐴𝑘(𝜈). Write 𝑐𝑖 = 𝑦𝑛𝑗 + 𝑧𝑛𝑘 and 𝑐𝑘 = 𝑥𝑛𝑖 + 𝑦′𝑛𝑗. Then
(𝜆 + 𝑥, 𝑦′ − 𝑦, −𝜈 − 𝑧) = 𝑢𝑟𝑗 + 𝑣𝑟𝑘.
From 𝑖 > 0, 𝑘 < 0 , and |𝑗| < 𝜌 𝑗, we get 𝑢 ≥ 0, 𝑣 ≥ 1 . The combination 𝑢 ≤ −1, 𝑣 ≥ 1 contradicts
𝑗 ≥ 𝜌𝑗 + 𝑎𝑗; 𝑢 ≤ −1, 𝑣 ≤ 0 contradicts 𝑖 > 0; and 𝑢 ≥ 0, 𝑣 ≤ 0 contradicts 𝑘 < 0.
Since 𝑖 ≤ 𝜌𝑖 + 𝑎𝑖 − 2, we have 𝑢 ≤ 1. Since −𝑘 ≤ 𝜌𝑘 + 𝑎𝑘 − 2, we have 𝑣 ≤ 1. Thus the only possibilities
are (𝑢, 𝑣) = (0, 1)or (1, 1).
Case A:
𝑐𝑖 = 𝑦𝑛𝑗 + (𝜌𝑘 − 𝜈)𝑛𝑘, 𝑐 𝑘 = (𝑏𝑖 − 𝜆)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑦 + 𝑎𝑗)𝑛𝑗. (AA-A)
Case B:
𝑐𝑖 = (𝑦′ + 𝑏𝑗)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑎𝑘 − 𝜈)𝑛𝑘, 𝑐 𝑘 = (𝜌𝑖 − 𝜆)𝑛𝑖 + 𝑦′𝑛𝑗. (AA-B)
The displayed coefficients come from the original genuine complement factorizations and are therefore
nonnegative. In particular, Case A implies 𝑏𝑖 − 𝜆 ≥ 0.
4.11.1 The singleton in the remaining direction 𝑆𝑗 is impossible
In Case A,
𝑊 = (𝜌𝑖 − 𝜆 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑎𝑗 + 𝑦 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝜌𝑘 − 𝜈 − 1)𝑛𝑘.
Use 𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘 = 𝜌𝑗𝑛𝑗 once in 𝑞𝑆𝑗
+ 𝑛𝑖:
𝑞𝑆𝑗
+ 𝑛𝑖 = (𝑏𝑖 − 𝜆)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑎𝑗 + 𝑦 − 𝜅𝑗 − 1 + 𝜌𝑗)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑎𝑘 − 𝜈 − 1)𝑛𝑘 ∈ 𝐻.
All coefficients are nonnegative, contradicting the missing direction 𝑖.
In Case B,
𝑊 = (𝜌𝑖 − 𝜆 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝜌𝑗 + 𝑦′ − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑎𝑘 − 𝜈 − 1)𝑛𝑘.
Since 𝜅𝑗 ≤ 𝜌𝑗 − 1, the element 𝑞𝑆𝑗
= 𝑊 − 𝜅𝑗𝑛𝑗 itself belongs to 𝐻, a contradiction.

Case A: if 𝑦 = 0 , then 𝑐𝑖 lies on the pure 𝑘-ray and is comparable with 𝑐𝑆𝑘
. Thus 𝑦 ≥ 1 . By antichain

The last coefficient is at least 𝜌𝑘 − 𝜈 ≥ 1, since 𝜅𝑘 ≤ 𝜌𝑘 − 1. This contradicts the missing direction 𝑖.
Case B: if 𝑦′ = 0, then 𝑐𝑘 lies on the pure 𝑖-ray and is comparable with 𝑐𝑆𝑖

The first coefficient is at least 𝑎𝑖 − 𝜆 ≥ 1, contradicting the missing direction 𝑘.
Thus two same-color arms cannot coexist with two singletons. Color reversal gives the corresponding
statement for two B-arms.
4.12 T wo arms of different colors and different directions: the two orientations
4.12.1 The orientation 𝐵𝑖(𝜆), 𝐴𝑘(𝜈)
Write 𝑐𝐵𝑖
= 𝑦𝑛𝑗 + 𝑧𝑛𝑘 and 𝑐𝐴𝑘
= 𝑥𝑛𝑖 + 𝑦′𝑛𝑗. Then
(𝜆 + 𝑥, 𝑎𝑗 − 𝑦 + 𝑦′, −𝑏𝑘 − 𝜈 − 𝑧) = 𝑢𝑟𝑗 + 𝑣𝑟𝑘.
The bounds 𝑖 > 0 , −𝑏𝑗 < 𝑗 < 𝜌 𝑗 + 𝑎𝑗, −𝑘 < 2𝜌 𝑘, and 𝑖 < 𝜌 𝑖 + 𝑏𝑖 force (𝑢, 𝑣) = (0, 1): mixed signs
contradict either the 𝑗-bound or 𝑖 > 0 ; 𝑢 ≥ 1, 𝑣 = 1 gives 𝑗 ≤ −𝑏 𝑗; 𝑢 ≥ 1, 𝑣 ≥ 2 gives 𝑖 ≥ 𝜌 𝑖 + 𝑏𝑖; and
𝑢 = 0, 𝑣 ≥ 2 gives −𝑘 ≥ 2𝜌𝑘.
Hence, with positive integers 𝛽 = 𝑏𝑖 − 𝜆, 𝛼 = 𝑎𝑘 − 𝜈, and 𝑅 = 𝑦 = 𝑦 ′ ≥ 0,
𝑐𝐵𝑖
= 𝑅𝑛𝑗 + 𝛼𝑛𝑘, 𝑐 𝐴𝑘
= 𝛽𝑛𝑖 + 𝑅𝑛𝑗. (BA)
An exact expression for 𝑊 is
𝑊 = (𝑎𝑖 + 𝛽 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑏𝑘 + 𝛼 − 1)𝑛𝑘
If 𝑅 = 0, however, this expression is signed. We call only the following completed expression actual:
𝑊 = (𝛽 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 + 𝜌𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝛼 − 1)𝑛𝑘. (BA-W)
It is obtained from 𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘 = 𝜌𝑗𝑛𝑗 and has all coefficients nonnegative.
If 𝑆𝑗 existed, then 𝜅𝑗 ≤ 𝜌𝑗 − 1, and (BA-W) would imply 𝑞𝑆𝑗
∈ 𝐻, a contradiction. Hence any singleton
must be in direction 𝑖 or 𝑘.
4.12.2 The opposite orientation 𝐴𝑖(𝜆), 𝐵𝑘(𝜈)
Similarly, the kernel vector is
(𝜆 + 𝑥, −𝑎𝑗 − 𝑦 + 𝑦′, 𝑏𝑘 − 𝜈 − 𝑧) = 𝑢𝑟𝑗 + 𝑣𝑟𝑘.
The bounds 𝑖 > 0 , −𝜌𝑗 − 𝑎𝑗 < 𝑗 < 𝑏 𝑗, −𝜌𝑘 < 𝑘 < 𝑏 𝑘, and 𝑖 < 𝜌 𝑖 + 𝑎𝑖 force (𝑢, 𝑣) = (1, 1). Mixed
signs violate the 𝑗- or 𝑘-bound; 𝑢 = 0 or 𝑣 = 0 violates the 𝑘-bound; 𝑢 ≥ 2 violates the 𝑖-bound; and 𝑣 ≥ 2
violates the 𝑘-bound.
Thus, with 𝑃 = 𝜌 𝑖 − 𝜆, 𝑇 = 𝜌 𝑘 − 𝜈, 𝑦 ≥ 0, and 𝑦′ = 𝑦 + 𝑎𝑗 − 𝑏𝑗 ≥ 0,
𝑐𝐴𝑖
= 𝑦𝑛𝑗 + 𝑇 𝑛𝑘, 𝑐 𝐵𝑘
= 𝑃 𝑛𝑖 + 𝑦′𝑛𝑗,

Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
𝑊 = (𝑃 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑎𝑗 + 𝑦 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 1)𝑛𝑘. (AB-reverse)
Assume 𝑆𝑖 exists. If 𝑦′ = 0 , then there is pure-ray comparability, so 𝑦′ ≥ 1 . Since 𝑎𝑗 + 𝑦 − 1 =
𝑏𝑗 + 𝑦′ − 1 ≥ 𝑏𝑗 and 𝑇 − 1 ≥ 𝑎 𝑘, we may replace 𝑏𝑗𝑛𝑗 + 𝑎𝑘𝑛𝑘 by 𝜌𝑖𝑛𝑖:
𝑊 = (𝑃 + 𝜌𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑦′ − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑏𝑘 − 𝜈 − 1)𝑛𝑘.
Since 𝜅𝑖 ≤ 𝜌𝑖 − 1, this gives 𝑞𝑆𝑖
∈ 𝐻, a contradiction.
Assume 𝑆𝑘 exists. If 𝑦 = 0, there is pure-ray comparability, so𝑦 ≥ 1. Since 𝑃 −1 ≥ 𝑏𝑖 and 𝑎𝑗+𝑦−1 ≥ 𝑎𝑗,
replace 𝑏𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑎𝑗𝑛𝑗 by 𝜌𝑘𝑛𝑘:
𝑊 = (𝑎𝑖 − 𝜆 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑦 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 + 𝜌𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘.
Since 𝜅𝑘 ≤ 𝜌𝑘 − 1, this gives 𝑞𝑆𝑘
∈ 𝐻, again a contradiction.
Thus the only singleton compatible with this opposite orientation is 𝑆𝑗.
4.13 If three singletons exist, then |𝑄| = 3
Assume 𝑆𝑖, 𝑆𝑗, 𝑆𝑘 exist. By § 4.2, every factorization of 𝑊 has all coordinates at most 𝜅𝑟 − 1 < 𝜌 𝑟. By
critical-box uniqueness §4.3, 𝑊 has a unique factorization. Since each maximal coordinate 𝜅𝑟 −1 is attained,
𝑊 = (𝜅𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝜅𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝜅𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘.
For any other row 𝑞, antichain incomparability forces every coordinate of its complement 𝑐 to be at most
𝜅𝑟 − 1. Thus any genuine factorization of 𝑐 is contained coefficientwise in this same factorization of 𝑊, and
hence 𝑞 = 𝑊 − 𝑐 ∈ 𝐻 , a contradiction.
No global factorization uniqueness is assumed here. The singleton caps first confine all factorizations to
the critical box, and uniqueness then follows from § 4.3.
4.14 Excluding three same-color arms and connecting to the classification
To exclude three arms of the same color, we follow the same tail pseudo-Frobenius element in the same
semigroup. Let the color be 𝜀, and write the actual arms in the three directions as
𝑞𝑖 = 𝑓𝜀 − 𝜆𝑖𝑛𝑖 ∈ 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ), 1 ≤ 𝜆 𝑖 ≤ 𝛼𝑖 − 1
Here 𝛼𝑖 is 𝑎𝑖 for color A and 𝑏𝑖 for color B. Choose, among nonnegative representations of 𝑓𝜀, one with the
least positive coefficient of 𝑚. Then
𝑓𝜀 = 𝑘𝑚 + ∑
𝑖
(𝑝𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖, 1 ≤ 𝑝 𝑖 ≤ 𝜆𝑖 ≤ 𝛼𝑖 − 1
The choice set is nonempty and the minimum exists by well-ordering of the positive integers.
In §B.1–§B.4 of Appendix B, the three rows 𝑈𝑖 representing the socle and the point 𝑝 determine a tetrahe-
dron in the relative lattice 𝑝 + 𝐿𝑚. A lattice point at an intermediate level would give a positive 𝑚-coefficient
smaller than 𝑘, while a nonvertex lattice point on the top face would imply 𝑓𝜀 ∈ 𝐻 . Hence the tetrahedron
is empty and has relative volume 𝑘. If 𝑘 ≥ 2, White’s width-one theorem leaves only a partition of the four
vertices into two pairs. For a class in the quotient lattice and its companion, minimality forbids simultane-
ous positivity of every coordinate. Writing these failure conditions for both colors produces a nonnegative
weighted row vector 𝑣 and a positive integer 𝐷 with
𝑣 ⋅ 𝑛 = 𝑘𝐷𝑚, |𝑣| 1 ≥ 𝑘𝐷
contradicting 𝑛𝑖 > 𝑚. All six inequalities, weights, and coordinate sums for the two colors are recorded in
§B.5–§B.8. Therefore 𝑘 = 1.

The possibility that a path terminates without an operation that exits the box while remaining positive is

At a positive exit all coefficients are still nonnegative, and 𝑢𝑖 ≥ 𝑎𝑖 for some 𝑖. Hence 𝑢𝑖 − 1 ≥ 𝑎𝑖 − 1 ≥ 𝜆𝑖,
and removing 𝜆𝑖𝑛𝑖 from this single representation gives 𝑞𝑖 ∈ Γ, a contradiction. For color B, simultaneously
exchange the indices, all 𝑎/𝑏-parameters, the arm elements, and their ranges; the same argument applies.
Thus three arms of the same color cannot exist. T wo depths on the same arm, and a corner together with
an arm of the same color, are also incompatible by pseudo-Frobenius incomparability. Therefore, for each
color, there are at most two corner/arm rows. This conclusion, including the cases involving a corner, is used
in the four-row classification below.
4.15 Exhaustive classification of four rows
Choose four distinct actual 𝑄-rows. Let 𝑠 be the number of singletons, 𝑐 the number of corners, and 𝑎 the
number of arms.
Case with a corner. By §4.4, no singleton is present. A corner cannot coexist with an arm of the same color
by antichain incomparability. Hence if 𝑐 = 1, 𝑎 = 3 , the three arms all have the opposite color. Duplicates
of an arm are impossible, so all three directions occur. At this point we apply § B.1 from Appendix B.
Henceforth 𝑐 = 0. By § 4.13, 𝑠 ≤ 2.
𝑠 = 2, 𝑎 = 2 . By § 4.5.1, a matched pair allows a singleton in at most one direction. By § 4.11, two
same-color arms cannot coexist with two singletons. The opposite orientation in § 4.12.2 likewise allows a
singleton in only one direction. Therefore the orientation is that of § 4.12.1, with singletons in directions 𝑖, 𝑘.
The configuration is exactly
𝑆𝑖, 𝐵 𝑖(𝜆), 𝐴𝑘(𝜈), 𝑆𝑘
20

PAGE 22
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
which we call PATH.
𝑠 = 1, 𝑎 = 3. If the three arms have the same color, they occupy all three directions. Applying § 4.11.1 to
the two arms outside the singleton direction gives a contradiction. Thus the color distribution is 2 + 1.
If a matched pair were present, § 4.10.2 would exclude coexistence with the singleton. Hence no matched
pair occurs in this case.
Up to cyclic permutation and full color reversal, the remaining candidate
𝑆𝑖, 𝐴 𝑖(𝜆), 𝐵𝑖(𝜆), 𝐴𝑘(𝜈)
contains a matched pair in direction 𝑖 together with the singleton 𝑆𝑖, and is therefore already excluded by
§4.10.2. It need not be treated as an independent terminal branch.
If there is no matched pair, the three missing directions are all distinct. Write the two same-color arms as
𝐴𝑖, 𝐴𝑘 and the opposite-color arm as 𝐵𝑗. Applying the opposite-orientation pair § 4.12.2 to 𝐴𝑘, 𝐵𝑗 shows
that the only possible singleton is 𝑆𝑖. Thus the configuration is exactly
𝑆𝑖, 𝐴 𝑖(𝜆), 𝐵𝑗(𝜇), 𝐴𝑘(𝜈)
which we call TYPE II.
𝑠 = 0, 𝑎 = 4 . There are three directions, and each color contributes at most one copy of any given arm,
so in some direction 𝑖 both 𝐴𝑖 and 𝐵𝑖 occur. By § 4.10.1, the only additional arms are 𝐵𝑗 and 𝐴𝑘. Hence the
configuration is
𝐵𝑗(𝜇), 𝐴𝑖(𝜆), 𝐵𝑖(𝜆), 𝐴𝑘(𝜈)
which we call CHAIN.
Thus every actual four-row nonsymmetric counterexample reduces exhaustively to PATH, TYPE II, or
CHAIN. The two coexistence restrictions for a matched pair were proved in § 4.10.1–§4.10.2.
4.16 Extraction of the scalar input needed for the terminal proofs
4.16.1 General singleton saturation lemma
Suppose the same actual 𝑊 has the representation
𝑊 = (𝑃 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 1)𝑛𝑘, 1 ≤ 𝑃 ≤ 𝜌 𝑖, 1 ≤ 𝑅 ≤ 𝜌 𝑗, 1 ≤ 𝑇 ≤ 𝜌 𝑘 (BOX-W)
and the complement of a singleton 𝑆𝑖 is 𝜅𝑛𝑖. By § 4.2, every factorization of 𝑊 has 𝑖-coordinate at most
𝜅 − 1 < 𝜌𝑖, and the maximum 𝜅 − 1 is attained.
Let the difference between an arbitrary factorization of𝑊 and (BOX-W) be𝑢𝑟𝑗+𝑣𝑟𝑘. If 𝑢 ≥ 1, 𝑣 ≤ 0, then
the 𝑗-coordinate is at most 𝑅 − 1 − 𝜌𝑗 < 0. If 𝑢 ≤ 0, 𝑣 ≥ 1, then the 𝑘-coordinate is at most 𝑇 − 1 − 𝜌𝑘 < 0.
If 𝑢, 𝑣 ≥ 1, then the 𝑖-coordinate is at least 𝑃 − 1 + 𝜌𝑖 ≥ 𝜌𝑖, contradicting the singleton cap. In the remaining
case 𝑢, 𝑣 ≤ 0, the 𝑖-coordinate is at most 𝑃 − 1.
Hence the maximum is 𝑃 − 1, so
𝜅 = 𝑃 .
The cyclic versions are identical. No global uniqueness of the factorization fiber is needed.
4.16.2 The full canonical positive box for CHAIN
Take the CHAIN rows of § 4.15. The matched pair in direction 𝑖 gives (MATCH). Apply § 4.12.1, in cyclic
order (𝑗, 𝑘, 𝑖), to the right-oriented pair 𝐵𝑗(𝜇), 𝐴𝑖(𝜆). Then
𝑐𝐴𝑖
= (𝑏𝑗 − 𝜇)𝑛𝑗 + 𝑇 𝑛𝑘, 𝑐 𝐵𝑗
= (𝑎𝑖 − 𝜆)𝑛𝑖 + 𝑇 𝑛𝑘.
Comparison with the unique 𝑗/𝑘-representation of the matched complement gives
𝑔 = 𝑏𝑗 − 𝜇 ≥ 1, 𝑅 = 𝜌 𝑗 − 𝜇.

𝛼 = 𝑎𝑘 − 𝜈 ≥ 1, 𝑇 = 𝜌 𝑘 − 𝜈,
𝑐𝐴𝑘
= (𝑏𝑖 − 𝜆)𝑛𝑖 + 𝑅𝑛𝑗.

4.16.3 Full input for TYPE II
Take 𝑆𝑖, 𝐴𝑖(𝜆), 𝐵𝑗(𝜇), 𝐴𝑘(𝜈). Compare Case A/B of § 4.11 for 𝐴𝑖, 𝐴𝑘 with §4.12.1 for 𝐵𝑗, 𝐴𝑖. In Case B,
the 𝑗-coordinate of 𝑐𝐴𝑖
would be simultaneously 𝑏𝑗 + 𝑦′ and 𝑏𝑗 − 𝜇, forcing 𝑦′ = −𝜇 < 0, impossible. Hence
Case A holds.
Consequently
𝜆 ≤ 𝑏𝑖, 𝑃 = 𝜌 𝑖 − 𝜆, 𝑅 = 𝜌 𝑗 − 𝜇, 𝑇 = 𝜌 𝑘 − 𝜈,

= (𝑎𝑖 − 𝜆)𝑛𝑖 + 𝑇 𝑛𝑘.
Thus the same 𝑊 has the form (BOX-W), and singleton saturation gives 𝑐𝑆𝑖
= 𝑃 𝑛𝑖. Therefore
𝑞𝑆𝑖
= −𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 1)𝑛𝑘.
The arm-domain restrictions 1 ≤ 𝜆 ≤ 𝑎 𝑖 − 1, 1 ≤ 𝜇 ≤ 𝑏 𝑗 − 1, and 1 ≤ 𝜈 ≤ 𝑎 𝑘 − 1, together with 𝜆 ≤ 𝑏𝑖,

4.16.4 Canonical endpoint rays for PATH
From the PATH pair 𝐵𝑖(𝜆), 𝐴𝑘(𝜈), §4.12.1 gives 𝛼 = 𝑎𝑘 − 𝜈 > 0 , 𝛽 = 𝑏 𝑖 − 𝜆 > 0, and 𝑅 ≥ 0. If 𝑅 = 0,
then 𝑐𝐴𝑘
= 𝛽𝑛 𝑖 is comparable on the pure ray with 𝑐𝑆𝑖
, or is the same row, which is impossible. Hence
𝑅 ≥ 1.
The missing-𝑖 critical box for 𝑐𝐵𝑖
gives 𝑅 < 𝜌𝑗. Thus
𝑃 = 𝑎 𝑖 + 𝛽 = 𝜌𝑖 − 𝜆, 𝑇 = 𝑏 𝑘 + 𝛼 = 𝜌𝑘 − 𝜈,

has the form (BOX-W). Applying singleton saturation in directions 𝑖 and 𝑘 gives
𝑐𝑆𝑖
= 𝑃 𝑛𝑖, 𝑐 𝑆𝑘
= 𝑇 𝑛𝑘.
If we set 𝑞𝐿 = 𝑞𝑆𝑖
, 𝑞𝐴 = 𝑞𝐴𝑘
, 𝑞𝐵 = 𝑞𝐵𝑖
, 𝑞𝑅 = 𝑞𝑆𝑘

This actual PATH ladder is passed to P5. Here 𝑞𝐴/𝑞𝐵 are only ladder labels and do not refer to the Herzog

5 Exclusion of the path configuration
We fix the same 𝑊 and the four elements of the path configuration from the preceding section. We divide
according to whether the returns of the two central elements match. In the nonmatching case, we first de-
termine the signs and then compare the returns at the two endpoints. In this section, 𝑞𝐴, 𝑞𝐵 label 𝐴𝑘, 𝐵𝑖,
respectively.
5.1 Exact input and labels
Take the PATH configuration of § 4.16.4, with 1 ≤ 𝜆 < 𝑏 𝑖, 1 ≤ 𝜈 < 𝑎 𝑘, 𝛽 = 𝑏 𝑖 − 𝜆 ≥ 1, 𝛼 = 𝑎𝑘 − 𝜈 ≥ 1 ,
𝑃 = 𝑎 𝑖 + 𝛽, 𝑇 = 𝑏𝑘 + 𝛼, 1 ≤ 𝑅 < 𝜌𝑗. The four rows for the same W are
𝑞𝐿 = −𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 1)𝑛𝑘, 𝑐 𝐿 = 𝑃 𝑛𝑖,
𝑞𝐴 = (𝑎𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 − 𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 1)𝑛𝑘 = 𝐴𝑘(𝜈), 𝑐𝐴 = 𝛽𝑛𝑖 + 𝑅𝑛𝑗,
𝑞𝐵 = (𝑃 − 1)𝑛𝑖 − 𝑛𝑗 + (𝑏𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘 = 𝐵𝑖(𝜆), 𝑐𝐵 = 𝑅𝑛𝑗 + 𝛼𝑛𝑘,
𝑞𝑅 = (𝑃 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 1)𝑛𝑗 − 𝑛𝑘, 𝑐 𝑅 = 𝑇 𝑛𝑘.
All four are actual pseudo-Frobenius rows, and 𝑆𝐻(𝑞𝐿) = {𝑖}, 𝑆𝐻(𝑞𝑅) = {𝑘}. The ladder is
𝑞𝐿 + 𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑖 = 𝑞𝐴 + 𝑅𝑛𝑗, 𝑞 𝐴 + 𝛽𝑛𝑖 = 𝑞𝐵 + 𝛼𝑛𝑘, 𝑞 𝑅 + 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘 = 𝑞𝐵 + 𝑅𝑛𝑗.
In this section the labels 𝑞𝐴, 𝑞𝐵 refer to the ladder; their missing directions are 𝑘, 𝑖, respectively.
5.2 The required part of the central match-or-seam argument
By §2.6, both elements 𝑞𝐴 + 𝑚, 𝑞𝐵 + 𝑚 lie in 𝐻. We call a pair satisfying
𝑞𝐴 + 𝑚 = 𝑋𝑛𝑖 + 𝐻0𝑛𝑗 + (𝑍 + 𝛼)𝑛𝑘,
𝑞𝐵 + 𝑚 = (𝑋 + 𝛽)𝑛𝑖 + 𝐻0𝑛𝑗 + 𝑍𝑛𝑘, 𝑋, 𝐻 0, 𝑍 ≥ 0
(P5-PAIR)
a PAIR. If no PAIR exists, then every factorization of the first element has 𝑘-coefficient below 𝛼, and every
factorization of the second has 𝑖-coefficient below 𝛽. Otherwise, the central ladder equality produces a PAIR.
Within the same actual element, replace HCR packets until the two factorizations are normalized as
𝑞𝐴 + 𝑚 = 𝑋𝑛𝑖 + 𝐻0𝑛𝑗 + 𝐾𝑛𝑘, 0 ≤ 𝑋 < 𝜌 𝑖, 0 ≤ 𝐻 0 < 𝜌𝑗, 0 ≤ 𝐾 < 𝛼,
𝑞𝐵 + 𝑚 = 𝑌 𝑛𝑖 + 𝐽0𝑛𝑗 + 𝐿𝑛𝑘, 0 ≤ 𝑌 < 𝛽, 0 ≤ 𝐽 0 < 𝜌𝑗, 0 ≤ 𝐿 < 𝜌 𝑘
23

PAGE 25
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
For the first element, every removal of a 𝑗-packet decreases 𝐻0 by 𝜌𝑗. If 𝑖 ≥ 𝜌 𝑖, an 𝑅𝑖-replacement gives
𝑘 ≥ 𝑎 𝑘 > 𝛼 and hence a PAIR. For the second element, remove 𝑗-packets; if 𝑘 ≥ 𝜌 𝑘, an 𝑅𝑘-replacement
gives 𝑖 ≥ 𝑏 𝑖 > 𝛽 and hence a PAIR. Thus, in the no-PAIR case, the process terminates within the displayed
ranges.
Comparison gives
𝐴𝑐𝑛𝑖 + (𝐻0 − 𝐽0)𝑛𝑗 = 𝐵𝑐𝑛𝑘, 𝐴 𝑐 = 𝑋 + 𝛽 − 𝑌 > 0, 𝐵 𝑐 = 𝛼 + 𝐿 − 𝐾 > 0.
If 𝐻0 < 𝐽0, let 𝑒 = 𝐽0 − 𝐻0 ∈ [1, 𝜌𝑗 − 1]; then 𝐴𝑐 ≥ 𝜌𝑖. Put 𝑎 = 𝐴𝑐 − 𝜌𝑖 ∈ [0, 𝛽 − 1]. Comparison after
using 𝑅𝑖 gives
𝑎𝑛𝑖 = (𝑒 − 𝑏𝑗)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐵𝑐 − 𝑎𝑘)𝑛𝑘.
Here 𝐵𝑐 − 𝑎𝑘 ≤ 𝑇 − 1 < 𝜌 𝑘. Split according to the four possible sign patterns of the two coefficients on
the right. If both are nonnegative, 𝑖-criticality forces all terms to vanish. If exactly one is negative, a positive
subcritical multiple of 𝑛𝑗 or 𝑛𝑘 is represented by the other two generators, a contradiction. Both negative is
impossible. Hence
𝐴𝑐 = 𝜌𝑖, 𝐽 0 − 𝐻0 = 𝑏𝑗, 𝐵 𝑐 = 𝑎𝑘.
This gives the following actual central kernel:
𝑞𝐴 + 𝑚 = (𝑌 + 𝑎𝑖 + 𝜆)𝑛𝑖 + 𝐻0𝑛𝑗 + 𝐾0𝑛𝑘,
𝑞𝐵 + 𝑚 = 𝑌 𝑛𝑖 + (𝐻0 + 𝑏𝑗)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐾0 + 𝜈)𝑛𝑘, (P5-PFREE-i)
0 ≤ 𝑌 < 𝛽, 0 ≤ 𝐾 0 < 𝛼, 0 ≤ 𝐻 0 < 𝑎𝑗.
The case 𝐻0 > 𝐽0 is sent to the same kernel by the full left-right reversal below. If 𝐻0 = 𝐽0, then 𝐴𝑐𝑛𝑖 =
𝐵𝑐𝑛𝑘. Criticality gives 𝐴𝑐 ≥ 𝜌𝑖, 𝐵𝑐 ≥ 𝜌𝑘, and after comparison with 𝑅𝑖, (𝐵𝑐 − 𝑎𝑘)𝑛𝑘 = (𝐴𝑐 − 𝜌𝑖)𝑛𝑖 + 𝑏𝑗𝑛𝑗
contradicts 0 < 𝐵𝑐 − 𝑎𝑘 ≤ 𝑇 − 1 < 𝜌 𝑘. Thus PAIR, 𝑃 𝐹 𝑅𝐸𝐸𝑖, and the full dual of 𝑃 𝐹 𝑅𝐸𝐸𝑖 exhaust the
possibilities. No endpoint reconstruction with unchecked signs is treated as actual here.

5.4 From PAIR to a strong port
Comparing PAIR with the canonical rows gives
𝑚 = (𝑋 − 𝑎𝑖 + 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝐻0 + 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑍 − 𝑏𝑘 + 1)𝑛𝑘.
Let 𝑡0 = 𝜌𝑗 − 𝐻0 − 1. Using HCR,
𝑡0𝑛𝑗 + 𝑚 = (𝑋 + 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑍 + 1)𝑛𝑘.
The right-hand side is greater than 𝑚, so 1 ≤ 𝑡0 < 𝜌𝑗. For the same 𝑞𝐴, 𝑞𝐵,
𝑞𝐴 = 𝑚 + (𝑎𝑖 − 𝑋 − 2)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑡0 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 𝑍 − 2)𝑛𝑘,
24

PAGE 26
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
𝑞𝐵 = 𝑚 + (𝑃 − 𝑋 − 2)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑡0 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑏𝑘 − 𝑍 − 2)𝑛𝑘.
If all final coefficients were nonnegative, the relevant gap would lie in Γ. Hence
𝑋 ≥ 𝑎 𝑖 − 1 ∨ 𝑍 ≥ 𝑇 − 1, 𝑋 ≥ 𝑃 − 1 ∨ 𝑍 ≥ 𝑏 𝑘 − 1.
On the other hand, if 𝑋 ≥ 𝑎 𝑖 − 1 and 𝑍 ≥ 𝑏𝑘 − 1, the root would be a nonnegative representation and would
put 𝑚 in 𝐻, impossible. Therefore
𝑋 ≤ 𝑎 𝑖 − 2, 𝑍 ≥ 𝑇 − 1 or 𝑋 ≥ 𝑃 − 1, 𝑍 ≤ 𝑏 𝑘 − 2.
By left-right reversal we may assume the first case. Then, with 𝑑 = 𝑎𝑖 − 1 − 𝑋, 𝑆 = 𝐻0 + 1, 𝑒 = 𝑍 − 𝑏𝑘 + 1,
𝑚 = −𝑑𝑛𝑖 + 𝑆𝑛𝑗 + 𝑒𝑛𝑘, 1 ≤ 𝑑 ≤ 𝑎 𝑖 − 1, 𝑆 ≥ 1, 𝑒 ≥ 𝛼
We call this identity WEAK-ROOT and pass it to the next proposition.
5.5 Normalization of a weak root and exclusion of PAIR
The input is the same 𝑞𝐿, 𝑞𝐴, 𝑊 from §5.1, together with a weak root from § 5.4.
5.5.1 Actual missing-direction returns
By the pseudo-Frobenius property, 𝑞𝐿 + 𝑛𝑗, 𝑞𝐿 + 𝑛𝑘 ∈ Γ. If a genuine factorization of 𝑞𝐿 + 𝑛𝑗 contained
𝑛𝑗, removing one copy from that same coefficient vector would give 𝑞𝐿 ∈ Γ. Thus its 𝑗-coefficient is zero.
Similarly, every genuine factorization of 𝑞𝐿 + 𝑛𝑘 has 𝑘-coefficient zero.
Moreover, by the SINGLETON condition neither successor lies in 𝐻, so the coefficient of m is positive.
Hence any genuine returns have the form
𝑞𝐿 + 𝑛𝑗 = ℓ𝑚 + 𝐴𝑛𝑖 + 𝐶𝑛𝑘, ℓ ≥ 1, 𝐴, 𝐶 ≥ 0, (LJ)
𝑞𝐿 + 𝑛𝑘 = 𝐿𝑘𝑚 + 𝐴𝑘𝑛𝑖 + 𝐵𝑘𝑛𝑗, 𝐿 𝑘 ≥ 1, 𝐴𝑘, 𝐵𝑘 ≥ 0. (LK)
No uniqueness, return-level minimality, or change of selected support is used here.
5.5.2 A weak ROOT forces 𝑆 < 𝑅 and 𝑒 ≥ 𝑇
Substitute WEAK-ROOT once into LK and use the exact identity for QA:
𝑞𝐴 = (𝐿𝑘 − 1)𝑚 + (𝐴𝑘 − 𝑑 + 𝑎𝑖)𝑛𝑖 + (𝐵𝑘 + 𝑆 − 𝑅)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑒 − 1)𝑛𝑘. (QA-ROOT)
Here
𝐿𝑘 − 1 ≥ 0, 𝐴 𝑘 − 𝑑 + 𝑎𝑖 ≥ 1, 𝑒 − 1 ≥ 0.
If 𝑆 ≥ 𝑅, the remaining coefficient is also nonnegative, giving 𝑞𝐴 ∈ Γ. Therefore
1 ≤ 𝑆 ≤ 𝑅 − 1. (S-W ALL)
Next, subtract the copy of 𝑚 represented by ROOT from the same 𝑊. The exact identity is
𝐹 = (𝑃 + 𝑑 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 𝑆 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 𝑒 − 1)𝑛𝑘. (F-ROOT)
The first two coefficients are nonnegative. If 𝑒 ≤ 𝑇 − 1 , the third is also nonnegative and 𝐹 ∈ Γ . Hence
𝑒 ≥ 𝑇 = 𝑏 𝑘 + 𝛼. (E-W ALL)
Thus, with 𝐾 ∶= 𝑒 − 𝛼, we have 𝐾 ≥ 𝑏𝑘.
The conclusion of this subsection applies to every ROOT satisfying only the WEAK-ROOT hypotheses.
Its original derivation from the paired-port coefficients 𝑋, 𝐻, 𝑍is no longer needed.

5.5.3 Finite ROOT normalization: 𝑏𝑘 ≤ 𝐾 < 𝜌 𝑘
By the preceding subsection, 𝐾 = 𝑒 − 𝛼 ≥ 𝑏 𝑘. If 𝐾 ≥ 𝜌 𝑘, use the HCR relation 𝜌𝑘𝑛𝑘 = 𝑏 𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑎𝑗𝑛𝑗 to
rewrite the same equality as
𝑚 = −𝑑′𝑛𝑖 + 𝑆′𝑛𝑗 + 𝑒′𝑛𝑘, 𝑑 ′ = 𝑑 − 𝑏𝑖, 𝑆 ′ = 𝑆 + 𝑎𝑗, 𝑒 ′ = 𝑒 − 𝜌𝑘 (ROOT-STEP)
We have 𝑒′ = 𝐾 − 𝜌𝑘 + 𝛼 ≥ 𝛼 and 𝑆′ ≥ 1.
If 𝑑′ ≤ 0 , the right-hand side is coefficientwise nonnegative, with 𝑆′ ≥ 1 , so 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 𝑗, contradicting
multiplicity.
Hence, in every surviving case, 𝑑′ > 0 and
1 ≤ 𝑑′ ≤ 𝑎𝑖 − 1, 𝑆 ′ ≥ 1, 𝑒 ′ ≥ 𝛼.
Thus all WEAK-ROOT hypotheses are preserved. The originalΓ, 𝐹 , 𝑚, 𝑊 , 𝑞𝐿, 𝑞𝐴 are unchanged, and LK/LJ
remain the same actual returns. Applying § 5.5.2 to the new ROOT gives
𝑆′ < 𝑅, 𝑒 ′ ≥ 𝑇 , 𝐾 ′ ∶= 𝑒′ − 𝛼 ≥ 𝑏𝑘
But
𝐾′ = 𝐾 − 𝜌𝑘 < 𝐾.
Since the positive integer 𝐾 strictly decreases, the process terminates. If no contradiction arises earlier, at
termination we have
𝑚 = −𝑑𝑛𝑖 + 𝑆𝑛𝑗 + (𝐾 + 𝛼)𝑛𝑘,
1 ≤ 𝑑 ≤ 𝑎 𝑖 − 1, 1 ≤ 𝑆 < 𝑅, 𝑏 𝑘 ≤ 𝐾 < 𝜌 𝑘
(NORMALIZED-ROOT)
We use 𝑑, 𝑆, 𝐾for the normalized variables from this point onward.
This does not assert that the original ROOT already had subcritical 𝐾. It is a finite normalization to
another ROOT preserving the same actual data, all hypotheses, and an explicit positive-integer measure. In
particular, no assumption 𝑏𝑖 > 𝑎𝑖 is made.
5.5.4 The endpoint missing- 𝑗 level is at least two
Fix any LJ. From 𝑞𝐿 + 𝑃 𝑛𝑖 = 𝑊,
𝐹 = (ℓ − 1)𝑚 + (𝑃 + 𝐴)𝑛𝑖 − 𝑛𝑗 + 𝐶𝑛𝑘.
Adding one copy of the HCR zero identity gives
𝐹 = (ℓ − 1)𝑚 + (𝛽 + 𝐴)𝑛𝑖 + (𝜌𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐶 − 𝑏𝑘)𝑛𝑘. (C-CERT)
Thus 𝐶 ≥ 𝑏𝑘 would put 𝐹 in Γ, impossible. Hence
0 ≤ 𝐶 ≤ 𝑏 𝑘 − 1. (C-CAP)
Now suppose ℓ = 1. Substituting the normalized ROOT into LJ gives the exact identity
𝑞𝐿 = (𝐴 − 𝑑)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑆 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐶 + 𝐾 + 𝛼)𝑛𝑘. (QL-CERT)
If 𝐴 ≥ 𝑑, all coefficients are nonnegative and 𝑞𝐿 ∈ Γ. Thus
0 ≤ 𝐴 ≤ 𝑑 − 1. (A-CAP)
On the other hand, comparing LJ with the canonical QL formula eliminates m completely and gives the
pure-H identity
(𝐶 + 𝐾 − 𝑏𝑘 + 1)𝑛𝑘 = (𝑑 − 𝐴 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 𝑆)𝑛𝑗 (PURE-K)
26

PAGE 28
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
The coefficients on the right satisfy
𝑑 − 𝐴 − 1 ≥ 0, 𝑅 − 𝑆 ≥ 1.
The positive integer 𝑉 ∶= 𝐶 + 𝐾 − 𝑏 𝑘 + 1 on the left satisfies
1 ≤ 𝐾 − 𝑏𝑘 + 1 ≤ 𝑉 ≤ 𝐾 < 𝜌 𝑘.
Thus 𝑉 𝑛𝑘 ∈ ⟨𝑛𝑖, 𝑛𝑗⟩ with 0 < 𝑉 < 𝜌 𝑘, contradicting Herzog criticality of 𝜌𝑘.
Therefore, for every genuine missing-𝑗 return of the same actual 𝑞𝐿,
ℓ ≥ 2. (ENDPOINT-LEVEL-2)
Criticality was used only in PURE-K, after the m-coordinate had been completely eliminated.
5.5.5 One-step Frobenius absorption
Insert the normalized ROOT once into LJ and use 𝑞𝐿 + 𝑃 𝑛𝑖 = 𝑊:
𝐹 = (ℓ − 2)𝑚 + (𝑃 + 𝐴 − 𝑑)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑆 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐶 + 𝐾 + 𝛼)𝑛𝑘. (LEFT-FABS)
The coefficients satisfy
ℓ − 2 ≥ 0, 𝑃 + 𝐴 − 𝑑 ≥ 𝛽 + 1 > 0, 𝑆 − 1 ≥ 0, 𝐶 + 𝐾 + 𝛼 > 0.
Thus LEFT-FABS is a genuine factorization of 𝐹 itself, so 𝐹 ∈ Γ , a contradiction.
Hence the strong-left weak-root configuration cannot occur.
5.6 Determining the sign in PFREE first
Starting from the central kernel 𝑃 𝐹 𝑅𝐸𝐸𝑖, put 𝜂 = 𝜈 − 𝑏 𝑘,
𝐴0 = 𝑌 + 𝜆 + 1, 𝐵 0 = 𝐻0 + 1, 𝐶0 = 𝑇 − 𝐾0 − 1,
𝐷0 = 𝑃 − 𝑌 − 1 > 0, 𝐸 0 = 𝐻0 + 𝑏𝑗 + 1, 𝐹0 = 𝐾0 + 𝜂 + 1
The same element 𝑚 has the two exact identities
𝑚 = 𝐴0𝑛𝑖 + 𝐵0𝑛𝑗 − 𝐶0𝑛𝑘 = −𝐷0𝑛𝑖 + 𝐸0𝑛𝑗 + 𝐹0𝑛𝑘. (P5-M)
They satisfy 𝐴0 + 𝐷0 = 𝜌 𝑖, 𝐸0 − 𝐵0 = 𝑏 𝑗, 𝐶0 + 𝐹0 = 𝑎 𝑘. Call the left representation M+ and the right
representation 𝑀 −. We first exclude 𝐹0 ≤ 0 . In that case 𝐶0 ≥ 𝑎 𝑘, 𝑇 = 𝐶 0 + 𝐾0 + 1 ≥ 𝑎 𝑘 + 1, and
𝑇 ≥ 𝑏 𝑘 + 1.
5.6.1 T wo genuine returns of the same right singleton
By the pseudo-Frobenius property and the missing directions,
𝑞𝑅 + 𝑛𝑖 = 𝑠𝑚 + 𝑈 𝑛𝑗 + 𝑉 𝑛𝑘, 𝑠 ≥ 1, 𝑈 , 𝑉 ≥ 0, (RIGHT-I)
𝑞𝑅 + 𝑛𝑗 = 𝑟𝑚 + 𝑋𝑛𝑖 + 𝑍𝑛𝑘, 𝑟 ≥ 1, 𝑋, 𝑍 ≥ 0. (RIGHT-J)
If the relevant missing coordinate were positive, removing one copy from that same factorization would
put 𝑞𝑅 in Γ. If the m-level were zero, the definition of the missing direction would be violated. Thus both
actuality and positivity of the levels are explicit.

5.6.2 𝑟 ≥ 2 or 𝑠 ≥ 2 gives a one-step absorption of F
Insert one copy of M+ into RIGHT-J and return to 𝑞𝑅 + 𝑇 𝑛𝑘 = 𝑊:
𝐹 = (𝑟 − 2)𝑚 + (𝑋 + 𝐴0)𝑛𝑖 + 𝐻0𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 + 𝑍 − 𝐶0)𝑛𝑘. (J-FABS)
If 𝑟 ≥ 2, every coefficient is nonnegative. In particular, 𝑇 + 𝑍 − 𝐶0 = 𝐾0 + 1 + 𝑍 ≥ 1.
Similarly, RIGHT-I gives
𝐹 = (𝑠 − 2)𝑚 + (𝐴0 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑈 + 𝐻0 + 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 + 𝑉 − 𝐶0)𝑛𝑘. (I-FABS)
If 𝑠 ≥ 2 , then 𝐴0 − 1 ≥ 0 , 𝑇 + 𝑉 − 𝐶 0 = 𝐾 0 + 1 + 𝑉 ≥ 1 , and the remaining coefficients are also
nonnegative.
Thus any surviving counterexample must satisfy
𝑟 = 𝑠 = 1. (UNIT)
5.6.3 T wo endpoint caps
From RIGHT-I and 𝑐𝑅 = 𝑇 𝑛𝑘,
𝐹 = (𝑠 − 1)𝑚 − 𝑛𝑖 + 𝑈 𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 + 𝑉 )𝑛𝑘.
Add the zero identity 𝜌𝑖𝑛𝑖 − 𝑏𝑗𝑛𝑗 − 𝑎𝑘𝑛𝑘 = 0 once:
𝐹 = (𝑠 − 1)𝑚 + (𝜌𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑈 − 𝑏𝑗)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 + 𝑉 − 𝑎𝑘)𝑛𝑘. (U-CERT)
Since 𝑇 ≥ 𝑎 𝑘, the inequality 𝑈 ≥ 𝑏 𝑗 would make all coefficients nonnegative. Hence
0 ≤ 𝑈 ≤ 𝑏 𝑗 − 1. (U-CAP)
For RIGHT-J, use 𝑅𝑗 as a completed repair:
𝐹 = (𝑟 − 1)𝑚 + (𝑋 − 𝑎𝑖)𝑛𝑖 + (𝜌𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 + 𝑍 − 𝑏𝑘)𝑛𝑘. (X-CERT)
Since 𝑇 ≥ 𝑏 𝑘,
0 ≤ 𝑋 ≤ 𝑎 𝑖 − 1. (X-CAP)
Only the non-strict part of the stronger T-CAPS is needed here.
5.6.4 Equal unit levels contradict pure-H criticality
With 𝑟 = 𝑠 = 1 , eliminate m completely from the two returns:
(𝑋 + 1)𝑛𝑖 = (𝑈 + 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑉 − 𝑍)𝑛𝑘. (UNIT-DIFF)
• If 𝑉 ≥ 𝑍 , the right-hand side is nonnegative and positive, whereas 0 < 𝑋 +1 ≤ 𝑎 𝑖 < 𝜌𝑖, contradicting
𝑖-criticality.
• If 𝑉 < 𝑍 , rewrite the equation as (𝑈 + 1)𝑛𝑗 = (𝑋 + 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑍 − 𝑉 )𝑛𝑘. Since 0 < 𝑈 + 1 ≤ 𝑏𝑗 < 𝜌𝑗,
this contradicts 𝑗-criticality.
All cases are eliminated. Therefore
PFREE𝑖 ∩ {𝐹0 ≤ 0} = ∅.

5.7 T wo-return absorption in PFREE and the level-one normal form
From now on 𝐹0 ≥ 1 has been proved. Take a return of the same left singleton,
𝑞𝐿 + 𝑛𝑗 = ℓ𝑚 + 𝐴𝑛𝑖 + 𝐶𝑛𝑘, ℓ ≥ 1, 𝐴, 𝐶 ≥ 0
Adding 𝑐𝐿 = 𝑃 𝑛𝑖 and comparing once with HCR gives
𝐹 = (ℓ − 1)𝑚 + (𝛽 + 𝐴)𝑛𝑖 + (𝜌𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐶 − 𝑏𝑘)𝑛𝑘
so 𝐶 ≤ 𝑏𝑘 − 1. A single substitution of 𝑀 −gives
𝑞𝐿 = (ℓ − 1)𝑚 + (𝐴 − 𝐷0)𝑛𝑖 + (𝐸0 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐶 + 𝐹0)𝑛𝑘
so 𝐴 ≤ 𝐷0 − 1. Furthermore,
𝐹 = (ℓ − 2)𝑚 + (𝑃 + 𝐴 − 𝐷0)𝑛𝑖 + (𝐸0 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐶 + 𝐹0)𝑛𝑘. (P5-PFREE-FABS)
Here 𝑃 + 𝐴 − 𝐷0 = 𝐴 + 𝑌 + 1 ≥ 1, 𝐸 0 − 1 ≥ 0, 𝐶 + 𝐹0 ≥ 1. Hence ℓ ≥ 2 is impossible.
For ℓ = 1, comparison with M+ gives
𝑃𝑐𝑛𝑖 = 𝑄𝑐𝑛𝑗 + 𝐾𝑐𝑛𝑘, 𝑃 𝑐 = 𝐴 + 𝐴0 + 1 ∈ [1, 𝜌𝑖],
𝑄𝑐 = 𝑅 − 𝐻0 − 1, 𝐾 𝑐 = 2𝑇 − 𝐾0 − 𝐶 − 2 ≥ 𝑇 .
If 𝑄𝑐 < 0, put 𝑟𝑐 = −𝑄𝑐 ∈ [1, 𝑎𝑗 − 1]. After applying 𝑘-criticality and then subtracting 𝑅𝑘,
(𝑃𝑐 − 𝑏𝑖)𝑛𝑖 = (𝑎𝑗 − 𝑟𝑐)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐾𝑐 − 𝜌𝑘)𝑛𝑘.
If 𝑃𝑐 ≤ 𝑏 𝑖, positivity is impossible; if 𝑃𝑐 > 𝑏 𝑖, then 0 < 𝑃 𝑐 − 𝑏𝑖 ≤ 𝑎 𝑖 < 𝜌 𝑖, contradicting criticality. If
𝑄𝑐 ≥ 0, 𝑖-criticality gives 𝑃𝑐 = 𝜌𝑖. Subtracting 𝑅𝑖 gives
(𝑄𝑐 − 𝑏𝑗)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐾𝑐 − 𝑎𝑘)𝑛𝑘 = 0.
If 𝑄𝑐 < 𝑏𝑗, then 0 < 𝑏𝑗 −𝑄𝑐 ≤ 𝑏𝑗 < 𝜌𝑗, contradicting 𝑗-criticality. If 𝑄𝑐 > 𝑏𝑗, then 0 < 𝑎𝑘 −𝐾𝑐 < 𝑎𝑘 < 𝜌𝑘,
contradicting 𝑘-criticality. Hence 𝑄𝑐 = 𝑏𝑗, 𝐾𝑐 = 𝑎𝑘, and moreover
𝐻0 = 𝑅 − 𝑏𝑗 − 1, 𝐴 = 𝐷 0 − 1, 𝐶 = 𝑇 − 𝐾 0 − 𝜂 − 2. (P5-FIX-J)
No uniform upper bound on 𝐾𝑐 − 𝑎𝑘 is needed in this comparison.
Take the other actual return 𝑞𝐿 + 𝑛𝑘 = 𝐿𝑚 + 𝐷𝑛𝑖 + 𝐵𝑛𝑗. Then
𝐹 = (𝐿 − 2)𝑚 + (𝑃 + 𝐷 − 𝐷0)𝑛𝑖 + (𝐵 + 𝐸0)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐹0 − 1)𝑛𝑘.
If 𝐿 ≥ 2 , all coefficients are nonnegative, a contradiction. Thus the only remaining case is the double-unit
configuration
𝑞𝐿 + 𝑛𝑗 = 𝑚 + 𝐴𝑛𝑖 + 𝐶𝑛𝑘,
𝑞𝐿 + 𝑛𝑘 = 𝑚 + 𝐷𝑛𝑖 + 𝐵𝑛𝑗,
𝐴, 𝐵, 𝐶, 𝐷 ≥ 0
(P5-DOUBLE-UNIT)
together with P5-FIX-J.
5.8 Exclusion of the double-unit case
The input consists of § 5.1, P5-M, P5-FIX-J, and P5-DOUBLE-UNIT. Below, FIX-J and UNIT-K refer to the
two returns above.

5.8.1 The C-cap and the upper bound for A
We have already proved in §5.7 that 0 ≤ 𝐶 ≤ 𝑏 𝑘 − 1. Also 𝐴 = 𝑃 − 𝑌 − 2 < 𝑃 < 𝜌 𝑖.
5.8.2 The two unit returns are pinned to one point
Eliminating m between (FIX-J) and (UNIT-K) gives
(𝐷 − 𝐴)𝑛𝑖 + (𝐵 + 1)𝑛𝑗 = (𝐶 + 1)𝑛𝑘. (EQ)
If 𝐷 ≥ 𝐴 , the right-hand 𝑘-coefficient satisfies 1 ≤ 𝐶 + 1 ≤ 𝑏 𝑘 < 𝜌 𝑘, while the left-hand side is
nonnegative and positive, contradicting 𝑘-criticality.
Therefore 𝐷 < 𝐴, and
(𝐵 + 1)𝑛𝑗 = (𝐴 − 𝐷)𝑛𝑖 + (𝐶 + 1)𝑛𝑘,
Thus 𝐵 + 1 ≥ 𝜌𝑗. Subtract 𝑅𝑗 once:
(𝐵 + 1 − 𝜌𝑗)𝑛𝑗 = (𝐴 − 𝐷 − 𝑎𝑖)𝑛𝑖 + (𝐶 + 1 − 𝑏𝑘)𝑛𝑘. (EQ-RJ)
Put 𝑥 = 𝐴 − 𝐷 − 𝑎𝑖. If 𝑥 > 0, then
𝑥𝑛𝑖 = (𝐵 + 1 − 𝜌𝑗)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑏𝑘 − 𝐶 − 1)𝑛𝑘,
whose right-hand side is nonnegative. Since 𝑥 < 𝐴 < 𝜌 𝑖, this contradicts 𝑖-criticality; if the right side were
zero, the equality 𝑥𝑛𝑖 = 0 would itself be impossible. If 𝑥 ≤ 0, then in (EQ-RJ) the left side is nonnegative
and the right side nonpositive, so equality is possible only if all coefficients vanish.
Thus the unique possibility is
𝐷 = 𝐴 − 𝑎𝑖, 𝐵 = 𝜌 𝑗 − 1, 𝐶 = 𝑏 𝑘 − 1. (PIN)
Up to this point, criticality has been applied only to pure-H relations after m has been completely eliminated.
5.8.3 T wo new exact roots
Comparing the value of 𝐶 in (FIX-J) with (PIN) gives
𝑇 − 𝐾0 − 𝜂 − 2 = 𝑏 𝑘 − 1 ⟹ 𝛼 = 𝐾 0 + 𝜂 + 1 = 𝐹 0.
Since 𝜂 = 𝜈 − 𝑏 𝑘,
𝑇 − 𝜈 = 𝛼 − 𝜂 = 𝐾 0 + 1 ≥ 1, (SURPLUS)
and
𝐶0 = 𝑇 − 𝐾0 − 1 = 𝜈.
Therefore (M+) becomes
𝑚 = 𝐴0𝑛𝑖 + (𝐻0 + 1)𝑛𝑗 − 𝜈𝑛𝑘, 𝐴 0 > 0. (ROOT+)
Comparing 𝐵 = 𝜌𝑗−1 in (UNIT-K) with the canonical expression for𝑞𝐿+𝑛𝑘, and writing 𝜇 = 𝜌𝑗−𝑅 ≥ 1,
gives
𝑚 = −(𝐷 + 1)𝑛𝑖 − 𝜇𝑛𝑗 + 𝑇 𝑛𝑘. (ROOT-T)
These are exact identities for the same 𝑚 and the same generators. The signed rows are not called actual
factorizations.

5.8.4 A missing-j return of the opposite singleton
Since 𝑞𝑅 ∈ 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ)and 𝑗 ∉ 𝑆𝐻(𝑞𝑅), take a genuine return
𝑞𝑅 + 𝑛𝑗 = 𝑟𝑚 + 𝑋𝑛𝑖 + 𝑍𝑛𝑘, 𝑟 ≥ 1, 𝑋, 𝑍 ≥ 0 (RIGHT-J)
If the 𝑛𝑗-coordinate were positive, removing one copy would give 𝑞𝑅 ∈ Γ , so it is zero. If 𝑟 = 0 , then
𝑞𝑅 + 𝑛𝑗 ∈ 𝐻, contrary to the missing direction.
5.8.5 𝑟 ≥ 2 gives a direct factorization of F
Insert one copy of 𝑚 from (ROOT+) into (RIGHT-J), remove one copy of 𝑛𝑗, add 𝑐𝑅 = 𝑇 𝑛𝑘, and remove
one copy of 𝑚 from 𝑊 = 𝐹 + 𝑚 . This gives
𝐹 = (𝑟 − 2)𝑚 + (𝑋 + 𝐴0)𝑛𝑖 + 𝐻0𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 + 𝑍 − 𝜈)𝑛𝑘. (RIGHT-FABS)
For 𝑟 ≥ 2, the coefficients satisfy 𝑟 − 2 ≥ 0, 𝑋 + 𝐴0 > 0, 𝐻0 ≥ 0, and 𝑇 + 𝑍 − 𝜈 ≥ 𝐾0 + 1 ≥ 1. Thus all
are nonnegative and 𝐹 ∈ Γ , a contradiction.
5.8.6 𝑟 = 1 gives a subcritical pure k/i relation
Using (ROOT+),
𝑞𝑅 = (𝑋 + 𝐴0)𝑛𝑖 + 𝐻0𝑛𝑗 + (𝑍 − 𝜈)𝑛𝑘.
If 𝑍 ≥ 𝜈 , then 𝑞𝑅 ∈ Γ. Hence
0 ≤ 𝑍 ≤ 𝜈 − 1. (RIGHT-Z)
Now insert (ROOT-T) into (RIGHT-J) with 𝑟 = 1 and compare with the canonical identity
𝑞𝑅 + 𝑛𝑗 = (𝑃 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + 𝑅𝑛𝑗 − 𝑛𝑘
Then
(𝑃 + 𝐷 − 𝑋)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 + 𝜇)𝑛𝑗 = (𝑇 + 𝑍 + 1)𝑛𝑘.
Since 𝑅 + 𝜇 = 𝜌𝑗 and 𝜌𝑗𝑛𝑗 = 𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘,
(𝑃 + 𝐷 + 𝑎𝑖 − 𝑋)𝑛𝑖 = (𝛼 + 𝑍 + 1)𝑛𝑘. (LAST)
The right coefficient satisfies
1 ≤ 𝛼 + 𝑍 + 1 ≤ 𝛼 + 𝜈 = 𝑎 𝑘 < 𝜌𝑘.
The right-hand side is positive, so the left coefficient is a positive integer. Thus (LAST) represents a positive
multiple of 𝑛𝑘 strictly below 𝜌𝑘 as a nonnegative multiple of 𝑛𝑖, contradicting 𝑘-criticality.
Both 𝑟 ≥ 2 and 𝑟 = 1 are impossible. Hence
𝔇𝑖 = ∅.
Applying §5.3 gives
𝔇𝑘 = ∅.
5.9 Closure of PATH
Section § 5.2 exhausts all actual central returns by PAIR or one of the two orientations of PFREE. PAIR is
excluded in § 5.4–§5.5. After the sign determination in § 5.6, PFREE is excluded in § 5.7–§5.8. The right-
oriented case is covered by exactly the same proof under the full parameter transformation § 5.3. Therefore
the PATH configuration supplied by G4 does not exist.

6 Exclusion of the one-singleton configuration (T ype II)
Independently of the preceding section, we now treat the configuration with one singleton from Section 4.
The input range includes 𝜆 ≤ 𝑏𝑖. The two missing-direction returns of the singleton give common coefficient
caps, after which we exhaust the three possible orderings of their levels.
6.1 Proposition excluding the one-singleton configuration
T6. The complete parameter range of § 4.16.3 is excluded by the following single two-return argument.
6.2 Exact input theorem
Let Γ = ⟨𝑚, 𝑛𝑖, 𝑛𝑗, 𝑛𝑘⟩ be a numerical semigroup, let 𝐹 be its Frobenius number, and let 𝐻 = ⟨𝑛𝑖, 𝑛𝑗, 𝑛𝑘⟩.
For positive integers 𝑎𝑟, 𝑏𝑟, with 𝜌𝑟 = 𝑎𝑟 + 𝑏𝑟, assume
𝜌𝑖𝑛𝑖 = 𝑏𝑗𝑛𝑗 + 𝑎𝑘𝑛𝑘, 𝜌 𝑗𝑛𝑗 = 𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘, 𝜌 𝑘𝑛𝑘 = 𝑏𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑎𝑗𝑛𝑗 (H)
and assume that each 𝜌𝑟 is the least positive critical multiplier of𝑛𝑟 with respect to the other two generators.
Let the integers 𝜆, 𝜇, 𝜈satisfy
1 ≤ 𝜆 ≤ 𝑎𝑖 − 1, 𝜆 ≤ 𝑏 𝑖, 1 ≤ 𝜇 ≤ 𝑏 𝑗 − 1, 1 ≤ 𝜈 ≤ 𝑎 𝑘 − 1 (RANGES)
Set
𝑃 = 𝜌 𝑖 − 𝜆, 𝑅 = 𝜌 𝑗 − 𝜇, 𝑇 = 𝜌 𝑘 − 𝜈, 𝑔 = 𝑏 𝑗 − 𝜇 ≥ 1.
For the same actual 𝐹 , 𝑚, assume
𝑊 = 𝐹 + 𝑚 = (𝑃 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 1)𝑛𝑘 (W)
The following singleton is an actual pseudo-Frobenius element with missing directions 𝑗, 𝑘:
𝑞𝑆 = −𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 1)𝑛𝑘 ∈ 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ), 𝑞𝑆 + 𝑛𝑗 ∉ 𝐻, 𝑞 𝑆 + 𝑛𝑘 ∉ 𝐻. (S)
Moreover,
𝑓𝐴 = (𝜌𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑎𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 − 𝑛𝑘 = (𝑎𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 − 𝑛𝑗 + (𝜌𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘,
𝑞𝐴𝑖
= 𝑓𝐴 − 𝜆𝑛𝑖 ∉ Γ, 𝑞 𝐴𝑘
= 𝑓𝐴 − 𝜈𝑛𝑘 ∉ Γ. (A-GAPS)
Theorem. No configuration satisfying this input exists.
The TYPE II configuration of § 4.16.3 satisfies these hypotheses. The proof does not require an actual
return of 𝐵𝑗, although that arm supplies the parameter 𝜇 and the canonical representation of 𝑊 above.
6.3 T wo actual singleton returns
Since 𝑞𝑆 ∈ 𝑃 𝐹 (Γ), 𝑞𝑆 + 𝑛𝑗, 𝑞𝑆 + 𝑛𝑘 ∈ Γ . In every factorization of either successor, the generator that
was added has coefficient zero. For example, for the generator 𝑛𝑗, if a factorization of 𝑞𝑆 + 𝑛𝑗 contained a
positive copy of it, removing one copy of 𝑛𝑗 from that same factorization would give 𝑞𝑆 ∈ Γ.
Therefore we may write
𝑞𝑆 + 𝑛𝑗 = ℓ𝑚 + 𝐴𝑛𝑖 + 𝐶𝑛𝑘, 𝑞 𝑆 + 𝑛𝑘 = ℎ𝑚 + 𝐷𝑛𝑖 + 𝐵𝑛𝑗 (SJ/SK)
32

PAGE 34
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
with 𝐴, 𝐵, 𝐶, 𝐷 ≥ 0 and ℓ, ℎ ≥ 1 . Positivity of the two 𝑚-levels follows from the missing-direction
assumptions: if one m-coefficient were zero, the corresponding successor would lie in 𝐻.
Comparison with the canonical singleton gives
ℓ𝑚 = −(𝐴 + 1)𝑛𝑖 + 𝑅𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 1 − 𝐶)𝑛𝑘, (JR)
ℎ𝑚 = −(𝐷 + 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 1 − 𝐵)𝑛𝑗 + 𝑇 𝑛𝑘. (KR)
6.4 Endpoint caps
Since 𝑞𝑆 + 𝑃 𝑛𝑖 = 𝑊, SJ gives
𝐹 = (ℓ − 1)𝑚 + (𝑃 + 𝐴)𝑛𝑖 − 𝑛𝑗 + 𝐶𝑛𝑘.
Adding −𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝜌𝑗𝑛𝑗 − 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘 = 0 gives
𝐹 = (ℓ − 1)𝑚 + (𝑏𝑖 − 𝜆 + 𝐴)𝑛𝑖 + (𝜌𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐶 − 𝑏𝑘)𝑛𝑘.
Because 𝜆 ≤ 𝑏𝑖, if 𝐶 ≥ 𝑏𝑘 all coefficients would be nonnegative and 𝐹 ∈ Γ . Hence
0 ≤ 𝐶 ≤ 𝑏 𝑘 − 1. (C-CAP)
Similarly, SK gives
𝐹 = (ℎ − 1)𝑚 + (𝑃 + 𝐷)𝑛𝑖 + 𝐵𝑛𝑗 − 𝑛𝑘
Adding −𝑏𝑖𝑛𝑖 − 𝑎𝑗𝑛𝑗 + 𝜌𝑘𝑛𝑘 = 0, we obtain
𝐹 = (ℎ − 1)𝑚 + (𝑎𝑖 − 𝜆 + 𝐷)𝑛𝑖 + (𝐵 − 𝑎𝑗)𝑛𝑗 + (𝜌𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘.
Since 𝑎𝑖 − 𝜆 ≥ 1,
0 ≤ 𝐵 ≤ 𝑎 𝑗 − 1. (B-CAP)
Up to this point, the repairs have been made only as signed identities; whenever a contradiction is inferred,
nonnegativity of every coefficient in the final expression is checked explicitly.
6.5 Uniform cap: 𝐴, 𝐷 ≤ 𝑏𝑖 − 1
Set
𝜂 = 𝜈 − 𝑏 𝑘, 𝜃 = 𝜇 − 𝑎 𝑗.
The canonical relations give
𝑓𝐴 − 𝑞𝑆 = 𝜇𝑛𝑗 + 𝜂𝑛𝑘. (ANCH-A)
Also,
𝑓𝐵 = (𝜌𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 − 𝑛𝑗 + (𝑏𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘, 𝑓 𝐵 − 𝑞𝑆 = 𝜃𝑛𝑗 + 𝜈𝑛𝑘. (ANCH-B)
No gap status for 𝑓𝐵 is assumed or used; ANCH-B is used only as an identity of values.
33

PAGE 35
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
6.5.1 𝜂 ≥ 1
Lifting SJ and SK to the same 𝑓𝐴 gives
𝑓𝐴 = ℓ𝑚 + 𝐴𝑛𝑖 + (𝜇 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐶 + 𝜂)𝑛𝑘,
𝑓𝐴 = ℎ𝑚 + 𝐷𝑛𝑖 + (𝐵 + 𝜇)𝑛𝑗 + (𝜂 − 1)𝑛𝑘.
Both are genuine factorizations. If 𝐴 ≥ 𝜆 or 𝐷 ≥ 𝜆 , removing 𝜆𝑛𝑖 from that same factorization would
give 𝑞𝐴𝑖
∈ Γ. Therefore
𝐴, 𝐷 ≤ 𝜆 − 1 ≤ 𝑏𝑖 − 1.
6.5.2 𝜃 ≥ 1
Combine 𝑞𝐴𝑘
= 𝑓𝐴 − 𝜈𝑛𝑘 = 𝑞𝑆 + 𝜇𝑛𝑗 − 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘 with SJ, and add ℛ𝑘 as a completed identity. This gives
𝑞𝐴𝑘
= ℓ𝑚 + (𝐴 − 𝑏𝑖)𝑛𝑖 + (𝜇 − 𝑎𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐶 + 𝑎𝑘)𝑛𝑘. (AK-J)
Hence 𝐴 ≥ 𝑏𝑖 would make all coefficients nonnegative, so 𝑞𝐴𝑘
∈ Γ. Thus 𝐴 ≤ 𝑏𝑖 − 1.
From SK the same operation gives
𝑞𝐴𝑘
= ℎ𝑚 + (𝐷 − 𝑏𝑖)𝑛𝑖 + (𝐵 + 𝜇 − 𝑎𝑗)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑎𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘. (AK-K)
If 𝐷 ≥ 𝑏𝑖, this is again a genuine factorization of the gap. Thus 𝐷 ≤ 𝑏𝑖 − 1.
Neither use of AK-J/AK-K assumes any sign for 𝜂.
6.5.3 𝜂 = 𝜃 = 0
From SJ,
𝑓𝐴 = ℓ𝑚 + 𝐴𝑛𝑖 + (𝜇 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + 𝐶𝑛𝑘
is genuine, so 𝐴 ≤ 𝜆 − 1 ≤ 𝑏𝑖 − 1.
In AK-K, 𝐵 + 𝜇 − 𝑎𝑗 = 𝐵 ≥ 0, so 𝐷 ≤ 𝑏𝑖 − 1 follows as well.
6.5.4 The remaining sign regions contradict singleton status
• If 𝜂 = 0, 𝜃 ≤ −1, then 𝜇 ≤ 𝑎𝑗 − 1, and
𝑞𝑆 + 𝑛𝑘 = (𝜌𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑎𝑗 − 𝜇 − 1)𝑛𝑗 ∈ 𝐻,
contradicting the missing direction 𝑘.
• If 𝜃 = 0, 𝜂 ≤ −1, then 𝜈 ≤ 𝑏𝑘 − 1, and
𝑞𝑆 + 𝑛𝑗 = (𝜌𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑏𝑘 − 𝜈 − 1)𝑛𝑘 ∈ 𝐻,
contradicting the missing direction 𝑗.
• If 𝜂, 𝜃 ≤ −1, then
𝑞𝑆 = (𝜌𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (−𝜃 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (−𝜂 − 1)𝑛𝑘 ∈ 𝐻 ⊆ Γ,
contradicting the gap status of 𝑞𝑆.
These integer sign cases are exhaustive. Therefore
0 ≤ 𝐴, 𝐷 ≤ 𝑏𝑖 − 1. (UNIFORM-I-CAP)

PAGE 36

6.6 Exhaustion of the return levels
6.6.1 ℓ = ℎ
Subtracting KR from JR gives
(𝐷 − 𝐴)𝑛𝑖 + (𝐵 + 1)𝑛𝑗 = (𝐶 + 1)𝑛𝑘.
If 𝐷 ≥ 𝐴, then 1 ≤ 𝐶 + 1 ≤ 𝑏 𝑘 < 𝜌𝑘 contradicts criticality of 𝑛𝑘. If 𝐷 < 𝐴, then
(𝐵 + 1)𝑛𝑗 = (𝐴 − 𝐷)𝑛𝑖 + (𝐶 + 1)𝑛𝑘
and 1 ≤ 𝐵 + 1 ≤ 𝑎𝑗 < 𝜌𝑗 contradicts criticality of 𝑛𝑗.
The coefficient of 𝑚 has been completely eliminated in this argument.
6.6.2 ℓ > ℎ
Substitute KR exactly once into the expression for 𝐹 obtained from SJ:
𝐹 = (ℓ − ℎ − 1)𝑚 + (𝑃 + 𝐴 − 𝐷 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 2 − 𝐵)𝑛𝑗 + (𝐶 + 𝑇 )𝑛𝑘. (ABS-L)
The coefficients satisfy
ℓ − ℎ − 1 ≥ 0, 𝑃 + 𝐴 − 𝐷 − 1 ≥ 𝑃 − 𝑏 𝑖 = 𝑎𝑖 − 𝜆 ≥ 1,
𝑅 − 2 − 𝐵 ≥ 𝑅 − 𝑎𝑗 − 1 = 𝑔 − 1 ≥ 0, 𝐶 + 𝑇 ≥ 0.
Thus every coefficient is nonnegative and 𝐹 ∈ Γ , a contradiction.
6.6.3 ℎ > ℓ
Conversely, substitute JR once into the expression for 𝐹 obtained from SK:
𝐹 = (ℎ − ℓ − 1)𝑚 + (𝑃 + 𝐷 − 𝐴 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝐵 + 𝑅)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 2 − 𝐶)𝑛𝑘. (ABS-R)
Again,
ℎ − ℓ − 1 ≥ 0, 𝑃 + 𝐷 − 𝐴 − 1 ≥ 𝑃 − 𝑏 𝑖 = 𝑎𝑖 − 𝜆 ≥ 1,
𝐵 + 𝑅 ≥ 0, 𝑇 − 2 − 𝐶 ≥ 𝑇 − 𝑏 𝑘 − 1 = 𝑎𝑘 − 𝜈 − 1 ≥ 0.
Hence 𝐹 ∈ Γ , a contradiction.
The three possible orderings have now been exhausted, so the input configuration does not exist.
TYPE II CLOSED
7 A unit root for the chain configuration
For the remaining chain configuration, we maximize the 𝑛𝑖-coordinate over the finite set of factorizations of
the same element ℎ𝐴 = 𝑞𝐴 + 𝑚. This yields a unit-root relation, after which we derive the returns, caps, and
slope inequalities needed for the boundary analysis. In this section 𝑞𝐴, 𝑞𝐵 denote 𝐴𝑖, 𝐵𝑖, respectively.

PAGE 37
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
7.1 Existence of a unit root
For the strict CHAIN configuration of §4.16.2, keeping the same Γ, 𝐹 , 𝑚, 𝑊and the same four actual pseudo-
Frobenius rows, and after a full color reversal if necessary, we obtain
𝑚 + 𝑑𝑛𝑖 = 𝑆𝑛𝑗 + 𝐶𝑛𝑘, 1 ≤ 𝑑 ≤ 𝜆, 𝑆 ≥ 𝑔 + 1, 𝐶 ≥ 𝛼
We call this conclusion CORE-ROOT.
7.2 Exact hypotheses
Let Γ = ⟨𝑚, 𝑛𝑖, 𝑛𝑗, 𝑛𝑘⟩, with 𝑚 < min(𝑛𝑖, 𝑛𝑗, 𝑛𝑘), let 𝐹 be the Frobenius number, and put 𝑊 = 𝐹 + 𝑚 .
The positive canonical Herzog data satisfy
𝜌𝑖𝑛𝑖 = 𝑏𝑗𝑛𝑗 + 𝑎𝑘𝑛𝑘, 𝜌 𝑗𝑛𝑗 = 𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘, 𝜌 𝑘𝑛𝑘 = 𝑏𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑎𝑗𝑛𝑗, 𝜌 𝑟 = 𝑎𝑟 + 𝑏𝑟. (HCR)
Let 𝜆, 𝛿, 𝛽, 𝑔, 𝛼 ≥ 1and set
𝑎𝑖 = 𝜆 + 𝛿, 𝑏 𝑖 = 𝜆 + 𝛽, 𝑃 = 𝜌 𝑖 − 𝜆 = 𝑎𝑖 + 𝛽 = 𝑏𝑖 + 𝛿, 𝑅 = 𝑎 𝑗 + 𝑔, 𝑇 = 𝑏 𝑘 + 𝛼.
Assume the same actual 𝑊 has the representation
𝑊 = (𝑃 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 1)𝑛𝑘 (W)
and that the following four elements are actual pseudo-Frobenius gaps:
𝑞𝐴 = (𝑃 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑎𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 − 𝑛𝑘,
𝑞𝐵 = (𝑃 − 1)𝑛𝑖 − 𝑛𝑗 + (𝑏𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘,
𝑞𝐽 = (𝑏𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 1)𝑛𝑗 − 𝑛𝑘,
𝑞𝐾 = (𝑎𝑖 − 1)𝑛𝑖 − 𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 1)𝑛𝑘. (CHAIN-PF)
By §4.6, the genuine factorization
𝑃 𝑛𝑖 = 𝐿𝑚 + (𝑡 + 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑢 + 1)𝑛𝑘, 𝐿 ≥ 1, 𝑡, 𝑢 ≥ 0. (PFIBER)
holds.
The proof of root existence itself uses only 𝑞𝐴 and PFIBER. The other three gaps are retained to preserve
the scope of the color-reversal argument and the downstream theorems.
7.3 An extremal choice on the same named actual element ℎ𝐴
Set
ℎ𝐴 = 𝑞𝐴 + 𝑚.
By the pseudo-Frobenius property, ℎ𝐴 ∈ Γ . Every Γ-factorization of ℎ𝐴 has m-coordinate zero: if it
were positive, removing one copy of 𝑚 from that same factorization would put 𝑞𝐴 in Γ. Hence ℎ𝐴 ∈ 𝐻 =
⟨𝑛𝑖, 𝑛𝑗, 𝑛𝑘⟩.
Among all H-factorizations of this same ℎ𝐴, choose one with maximal 𝑖-coordinate:
ℎ𝐴 = 𝑋𝑛𝑖 + 𝑌 𝑛𝑗 + 𝑍𝑛𝑘, 𝑋, 𝑌 , 𝑍 ≥ 0. (MAX-I)
Because all generators are positive, this factorization set is finite and nonempty, so the maximum exists
explicitly.
36

PAGE 38
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
7.3.1 𝑋 ≤ 𝑃 − 1
By PFIBER,
𝑃 𝑛𝑖 − 𝑚 = (𝐿 − 1)𝑚 + (𝑡 + 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑢 + 1)𝑛𝑘 ∈ Γ.
If 𝑋 ≥ 𝑃 , the same actual factorization of ℎ𝐴 contains 𝑃 𝑛𝑖 coefficientwise. Replacing that block by the
displayed representation gives
𝑞𝐴 = ℎ𝐴 − 𝑚 = (ℎ𝐴 − 𝑃 𝑛𝑖) + (𝑃 𝑛𝑖 − 𝑚) ∈ Γ,
a contradiction. Hence 𝑋 ≤ 𝑃 − 1 .
7.3.2 𝑌 ≤ 𝜌 𝑗 − 1, 𝑍 ≤ 𝜌𝑘 − 1
If 𝑌 ≥ 𝜌 𝑗, replace the contained block 𝜌𝑗𝑛𝑗 by 𝑎𝑖𝑛𝑖 + 𝑏𝑘𝑛𝑘. This produces another factorization of the same
ℎ𝐴 with 𝑖-coordinate 𝑋 + 𝑎𝑖 > 𝑋 , contradicting MAX-I.
If 𝑍 ≥ 𝜌 𝑘, the relation 𝑅𝑘 similarly produces a same- ℎ𝐴 factorization with 𝑖-coordinate 𝑋 + 𝑏 𝑖 > 𝑋 .
Therefore
0 ≤ 𝑋 ≤ 𝑃 − 1, 0 ≤ 𝑌 ≤ 𝜌 𝑗 − 1, 0 ≤ 𝑍 ≤ 𝜌 𝑘 − 1. (BOX)
No generic connectivity or uniqueness is used; only these two explicit legal replacements inside a factor-
ization of the same element are needed.
7.4 The initial root and its bounds
Comparing the canonical expression for 𝑞𝐴 with MAX-I, define
𝑑 = 𝑃 − 1 − 𝑋, 𝑆 = 𝑌 − 𝑎 𝑗 + 1, 𝐶 = 𝑍 + 1
Then exactly
𝑚 = −𝑑𝑛𝑖 + 𝑆𝑛𝑗 + 𝐶𝑛𝑘. (ROOT0)
From BOX,
0 ≤ 𝑑 ≤ 𝑃 − 1, 1 − 𝑎 𝑗 ≤ 𝑆 ≤ 𝑏 𝑗, 1 ≤ 𝐶 ≤ 𝜌 𝑘. (INITIAL)
At this point no nonnegativity assumption is made on 𝑆.
7.4.1 𝑆 ≥ 𝑔
Rewrite 𝑊 using 𝑅𝑘, and use 𝐹 = 𝑊 − 𝑚 together with ROOT0. This gives
𝐹 = (𝛿 + 𝑑 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑔 − 𝑆 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝜌𝑘 + 𝑇 − 𝐶 − 1)𝑛𝑘. (F-S)
If 𝑆 ≤ 𝑔 − 1, then all coefficients are nonnegative: 𝛿 + 𝑑 − 1 ≥ 0, 𝑔 − 𝑆 − 1 ≥ 0, and 𝜌𝑘 + 𝑇 − 𝐶 − 1 ≥
𝑇 − 1 ≥ 0 . Hence 𝐹 ∈ Γ , a contradiction.
Thus 𝑆 ≥ 𝑔 ≥ 1 .
37

PAGE 39
Canonical reduction and type T ype bound under canonical reduction
7.4.2 𝐶 ≥ 𝛼
Similarly, rewriting 𝑊 with 𝑅𝑗 gives
𝐹 = (𝛽 + 𝑑 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝜌𝑗 + 𝑅 − 𝑆 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝛼 − 𝐶 − 1)𝑛𝑘. (F-C)
If 𝐶 ≤ 𝛼 − 1, then 𝛽 + 𝑑 − 1 ≥ 0,
𝜌𝑗 + 𝑅 − 𝑆 − 1 ≥ 𝜌𝑗 + 𝑅 − 𝑏𝑗 − 1 = 𝑎𝑗 + 𝑅 − 1 ≥ 0,
and 𝛼 − 𝐶 − 1 ≥ 0. Hence 𝐹 ∈ Γ , a contradiction.
Therefore 𝐶 ≥ 𝛼 ≥ 1 .
7.4.3 𝑑 ≥ 1
If 𝑑 = 0, then 𝑚 = 𝑆𝑛𝑗 + 𝐶𝑛𝑘, with 𝑆, 𝐶 ≥ 1, so 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛𝑗 + 𝑛𝑘. This contradicts the multiplicity inequality
𝑚 < min(𝑛𝑗, 𝑛𝑘).
7.4.4 𝑑 ≤ 𝜆
From 𝐹 = 𝑊 − 𝑚 and ROOT0,
𝐹 = (𝑃 + 𝑑 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 − 𝑆 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 − 𝐶 − 1)𝑛𝑘.
Add one copy of 𝑅𝑖 as a completed zero identity:
𝐹 = (𝑑 − 𝜆 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑅 + 𝑏𝑗 − 𝑆 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑇 + 𝑎𝑘 − 𝐶 − 1)𝑛𝑘. (F-D)
If 𝑑 ≥ 𝜆 + 1, every coefficient is nonnegative. Indeed,
𝑅 + 𝑏𝑗 − 𝑆 − 1 ≥ 𝑅 − 1 ≥ 0,
and
𝑇 + 𝑎𝑘 − 𝐶 − 1 ≥ 𝑇 + 𝑎 𝑘 − 𝜌𝑘 − 1 = 𝑇 − 𝑏 𝑘 − 1 = 𝛼 − 1 ≥ 0.
Thus 𝐹 ∈ Γ , a contradiction.
We have proved
1 ≤ 𝑑 ≤ 𝜆, 𝑔 ≤ 𝑆 ≤ 𝑏 𝑗, 𝛼 ≤ 𝐶 ≤ 𝜌 𝑘. (ROOT-BOX)
Signed intermediate identities are not called actual; every contradiction above uses a final representation
of 𝐹 whose coefficients have been checked to be nonnegative.
7.5 At least one inequality is strict
If 𝑆 = 𝑔 and 𝐶 = 𝛼, substituting ROOT0 into 𝐹 = 𝑊 − 𝑚 gives
𝐹 = (𝑃 + 𝑑 − 1)𝑛𝑖 + (𝑎𝑗 − 1)𝑛𝑗 + (𝑏𝑘 − 1)𝑛𝑘 ∈ Γ,
a contradiction. Therefore
𝑆 ≥ 𝑔 + 1 or 𝐶 ≥ 𝛼 + 1. (STRICT)
If 𝑆 ≥ 𝑔+1, we are already in the desired orientation of CORE-ROOT. It remains to treat𝑆 = 𝑔, 𝐶 ≥ 𝛼+1,
where we apply the full color reversal below.
